, we also provide some derived rules for convenience in common scenarios. All the derivations are shown in
Section

C.3
.
The first rules pertain to conditional statements. These rules use
Prob Split
and
ND Split
to deconstruct the pre- and postconditions in order to analyze both branches of the conditional statement. These are similar to the rules found in Ellora
(
Barthe et al., 2018
)
and Outcome Logic
(
Zilberstein, 2024
)
.

\frac{\begin{subarray}{c}\varphi_{1}\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\hskip 8.19447pt\varphi_{2}\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lnot e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\end{subarray}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{1}\oplus_{p}\varphi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{if}~e~\texttt{then}~C_{1}~\texttt{else}~C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi_{1}\oplus_{p}\psi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\qquad\frac{\begin{subarray}{c}\varphi_{1}\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\hskip 8.19447pt\varphi_{2}\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lnot e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\end{subarray}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{1}\mathbin{\&}\varphi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{if}~e~\texttt{then}~C_{1}~\texttt{else}~C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi_{1}\mathbin{\&}\psi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}

In addition, if the precondition of the conditional statement is a basic assertion
P
, then we can use the typical conditional rule from Hoare logic. This relies on the Hahn decomposition theorem:
\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\land e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\mathbin{\&}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\land\lnot e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
, that is, if
\mu\vDash P
, then
\mu
can be separated into two portions where
e
is true and false, respectively. Due to idempotence, we can simplify the postconditions using the consequence
\psi\mathbin{\&}\psi\Rightarrow\psi
.

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\land\lnot e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{if}~e~\texttt{then}~C_{1}~\texttt{else}~C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}

Finally, we provide rules for analyzing the coin flip and nondeterministic selection syntactic sugar introduced in
Section

3.2
. The flip rule is derived using a straightforward application of
Prob
. The rule for nondeterministic selection is proven by induction on the size of
S
(recall that
S
is finite).

\frac{\varphi\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e=p\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\quad x\notin\mathsf{fv}(\varphi)}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x\coloneqq\mathsf{flip}(e)~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi\land(\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{p}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x=\mathsf{false}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\qquad\quad\frac{\;}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x\leftarrow S~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\textstyle\bignd_{v\in S}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x=v\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}

Although the precondition of the nondeterministic selection rule is
\mathsf{true}
, it can be used in conjunction with the rule of
Constancy
so that any basic assertion
P
can be the precondition. Beyond that, to extend to any precondition
\varphi
, de-structuring rules must be applied just like with the
Nondet
rule.

Remark 1 (Completeness).

We have not explored completeness of Demonic Outcome Logic, even for the loop-free fragment. One reason for this is that the derivations witnessed by the completeness proofs for similar probabilistic logics do not mimic the sort of derivations that one would produce by hand. For example, in Ellora
(
Barthe et al., 2018
)
, the completeness proof involves quantifying over the (infinitely many) distributions that could satisfy the precondition, then showing that a derivation of the strongest postcondition is possible given any fixed one of those distributions. The complexity comes not only from loops, but also purely sequential constructs like probabilistic branching and if statements. To see examples of where this complexity arises, see
Zilberstein (2024, Definition 3.5)
, or
Dardinier and
Müller (2024, Example 1)
.

5.
Analyzing Loops

In this section, we discuss proof rules for analyzing loops. We are inspired by work on weakest pre-expectations
(
McIver and Morgan, 2005
;
Kaminski, 2019
;
McIver
et al., 2018
)
, where probabilistic loop analysis has been studied extensively, but will argue in this section that our program logic approach has two advantages.

Fewer Conditions to Check

The weakest pre-expectation proof rules involve multiple checks, which include both
sub
-invariants and
super
-invariants and computing expected values of ranking functions. In contrast, in Demonic OL all the proof rules revolve around just one construct—outcome triples—and the premises of the rules can accordingly be consolidated.

Multiple Outcomes

It is often useful to specify programs in terms of their distinct outcomes, which we achieve using the assertions from
Section

4.1
. Pre-expectation calculi can only represent multiple outcomes by carrying out several distinct derivations, whereas Demonic OL can do so in one shot.

5.1.
Almost Sure Termination

As we mentioned in
Section

3.3
, our semantics based on the Smyth powerdomain is suitable for total correctness—specifications implying that the program terminates.
Since we are in a probabilistic setting, it makes sense to talk about a finer notion of termination—
almost sure termination
—meaning that the program terminates with probability 1. In terms of our program semantics, a program almost surely terminates if
\bot
does not appear in the support of any of its resultant distributions.

Definition 5.1 (Almost Sure Termination).

A program
C

almost surely terminates
on input
\sigma
iff

\forall\mu\in\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket(\sigma).~\bot\notin\mathsf{supp}(\mu)

In addition,
C

universally almost surely terminates
if it almost surely terminates on all
\sigma\in\Sigma
.

Going further, we show how almost sure termination is established in Demonic Outcome Logic.

Theorem 5.2.

A program
C
almost surely terminates starting from any state satisfying
P
if:

\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}

As a corollary,
C
universally almost surely terminates if
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
.

Proof.

The triple
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
means that if
\sigma\in\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis
, that is, if
\delta_{\sigma}\vDash\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
, then
\mathsf{supp}(\mu)\subseteq\llparenthesis\mathsf{true}\rrparenthesis=\Sigma
for all
\mu\in\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket(\sigma)
. Since
\bot\notin\Sigma
,
\mathsf{supp}(\mu)\subseteq\Sigma
iff
\bot\notin\mathsf{supp}(\mu)
.
∎

Following from
Theorem

5.2
, if
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
holds, then
C
almost surely terminates as long as
\psi\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
, which is simple to check in many cases. For example, if
\psi
is formed as a collection of atoms
Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{n}
joined by
\mathbin{\&}
and
\oplus_{p}
connectives, then the program almost surely terminates since
\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{i}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
holds trivially, and connectives can be collapsed using idempotence of
\mathbin{\&}
and
\oplus_{p}
.

5.2.
The Zero-One Law

McIver and Morgan (2005)
showed that under certain conditions, probabilistic programs must terminate with probability either 0 or 1. In this circumstance, almost sure termination can be established simply by showing that the program terminates with nonzero probability.

The original rule of
McIver and Morgan (2005, §2.6)
used a propositional invariant
P
to describe all reachable states after each iteration of the loop. We generalize their rule by using an outcome assertion
\varphi
as the invariant, so that in addition to describing which states are reachable, we can also describe how those reachable states are distributed. Our version of the rule is stated below.

\hypertarget{rule:Zero-One}{\frac{\varphi\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\quad\psi\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lnot e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi\mathbin{\&}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{while}~e~\texttt{do}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lnot e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{p}\top{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\quad p>0}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{while}~e~\texttt{do}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{\textsc{Zero-One}}}

Given some loop
\texttt{while}~e~\texttt{do}~C
, the first step of the
Zero-One
law is to come up with an invariant pair
\varphi
and
\psi
, where
\varphi
represents the distribution of states where the guard
e
remains true and
\psi
represents the distribution of states in which the loop has terminated. More precisely,
\varphi\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
and
\psi\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lnot e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
.
Next, we must prove that this is an invariant pair, by proving the following triple.

(3)

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi\mathbin{\&}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}

That is, if the initial states are distributed according to
\varphi
, then loop body
C
will reestablish
\varphi
with some probability
q
and will terminate (in
\psi
) with probability
1-q
. In the limit,
\psi
will describe the entire distribution of
terminating
outcomes (
Lemma

D.2
), although we do not yet know if the loop almost surely terminates.
We can establish almost sure termination if
\varphi
guarantees some nonzero probability of termination, as represented by the following triple.

(4)

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{while}~e~\texttt{do}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lnot e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{p}\top{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\qquad\text{where}\qquad p>0

That is, for every
\mu\vDash\varphi
, the loop will always terminate (with
\lnot e
holding) with probability at least
p>0
. From (
3
), we know that on the
i^{\text{th}}
iteration of the loop, there is some probability
q_{i}
of reestablishing the invariant
\varphi
, in which case the loop will continue to execute. This means that the total probability of nontermination is the product of all the
q_{i}
.
In general, it is possible for such an infinite product to converge to a nonzero probability, however, from (
4
) we know that every tail of that product must be at most
1-p
, which is strictly less than 1.

\mathbb{P}[\text{nonterm}]\quad=\quad q_{1}\times q_{2}\times q_{3}\times\cdots\times\underbrace{q_{n}\times q_{n+1}\times\cdots}_{\leq 1-p}\quad=\quad 0

This implies that the product of the
q_{i}
must go to
0
, so that the loop almost surely terminates.
The full soundness proof is available in
Section

D.1
.

5.3.
Proving Termination with Variants and Ranking Functions

Although the
Zero-One
law provides a means for proving almost sure termination, it can be difficult to use directly, because one still must establish a
minimum probability of termination
. In this section, we provide some inference rules derived from
Zero-One
that are easier to apply.
The first rule uses a bounded family of
variants

(\varphi_{n})_{n=0}^{N}
, where
\varphi_{0}\Rightarrow\lnot e
and
\varphi_{n}\Rightarrow e
for
1\leq n\leq N
. The index
n
can be thought of as a
rank
, so that we get closer to termination as
n
descends towards zero.
The premise of the rule is that the rank must decrease by at least 1 with probability
p>0
on each iteration. We represent this formally using the following triple.

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{n}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\textstyle(\bignd_{k=0}^{n-1}\varphi_{k})\oplus_{p}(\bignd_{k=0}^{N}\varphi_{k}){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}

The assertion
\bignd_{k=0}^{n-1}\varphi_{k}
is an aggregation of all the variants with rank strictly lower than
n
, essentially meaning that the states must be distributed according to those variants, but without specifying their relative probabilities. Note that,
e.g.,

\varphi_{n-1}\Rightarrow\bignd_{k=0}^{n-1}\varphi_{k}
, so it is possible to establish this assertion if the rank always decreases by exactly 1. So, the postcondition states that with probability
p
the rank decreases by at least 1. That means that
\bignd_{k=0}^{N}\varphi_{k}
must hold with probability
1-p
, meaning that the rank can
increase
too, as long as that increase is not too likely.

In order to establish a minimum termination probability, we note that starting at
\varphi_{n}
, it takes at most
N
steps to reach rank 0, therefore the loop terminates with probability at least
p^{N}
, which is greater than 0 since
p>0
and
N
is finite. Putting this all together, we get the following rule.

\hypertarget{rule:Bounded Variant}{\frac{\varphi_{0}\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lnot e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\quad\forall n\in\{1,\ldots,N\}.\;\;\varphi_{n}\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{n}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\textstyle(\bignd_{k=0}^{n-1}\varphi_{k})\oplus_{p}(\bignd_{k=0}^{N}\varphi_{k}){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\textstyle\bignd_{k=0}^{N}\varphi_{k}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{while}~e~\texttt{do}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{0}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{\textsc{Bounded Variant}}}

As a special case of the
Bounded Variant
rule, we can derive the variant rule of
McIver and Morgan (2005, Lemma 7.5.1)
. Instead of recording the rank with a family of outcome assertions, we will instead use an integer-valued expression
e_{\mathsf{rank}}
. This can be thought of as a ranking function, since
\left\llbracket e_{\mathsf{rank}}\right\rrbracket\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{Z}
gives us a rank for each state
\sigma\in\Sigma
. In addition, the propositional invariant
P
describes the reachable states after each iteration of the loop.
Finally, as long as the invariant holds and the loop guard is true,
e_{\mathsf{rank}}
must be bounded between
\ell
and
h
, in other words
\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\ell\leq e_{\mathsf{rank}}\leq h\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
. The premise of the rule is that each iteration of the loop must strictly decrease the rank with probability at least
p>0
. That is, for any
n
:

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land e\land e_{\mathsf{rank}}=n\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\land e_{\mathsf{rank}}<n\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{p}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}

Given that the rank is integer-valued and strictly decreasing, it must fall below the lower bound
\ell
within at most
h-\ell+1
steps, at which point
e
becomes false since
\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\ell\leq e_{\mathsf{rank}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
. So the loop terminates with probability at least
p^{h-\ell+1}
, and so by the
Zero-One
law, it almost surely terminates.
In
Section

D.2
, we show how this rule is derived from
Bounded Variant
by letting
\varphi_{0}\triangleq\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land\lnot e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
and
\varphi_{n}\triangleq\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land e\land e_{\mathsf{rank}}=\ell+n-1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
for
1\leq n\leq h-\ell+1
.
The full rule is shown below:

\hypertarget{rule:Bounded Rank}{\frac{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\ell\leq e_{\mathsf{rank}}\leq h\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\;\;\;\forall n.\;{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\land e\land e_{\mathsf{rank}}=n\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\land e_{\mathsf{rank}}<n\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{p}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{while}~e~\texttt{do}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\land\lnot e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{\textsc{Bounded Rank}}}

As an example application of the rule, recall the following resetting random walk, where the agent moves left with probability
\frac{1}{2}
, otherwise it resets to a position chosen by an adversary.

\begin{array}[]{l}\texttt{while}~x>0~\texttt{do}\\
\quad(x\coloneqq x-1)\oplus_{\frac{1}{2}}(x\leftarrow\{1,\ldots,5\})\end{array}

When starting in a state where
0\leq x\leq 5
, this program almost surely terminates. Although there are uncountably many nonterminating traces, the probability of nontermination is zero. Even in the worst case in which the adversary always chooses 5, the agent eventually moves left in five consecutive iterations with probability 1. In fact, the program terminates in finite expected time, as it is a Bernoulli process. Using the invariant
P\triangleq 0\leq x\leq 5
, the ranking function
e_{\mathsf{rank}}=x
, and the probability
p=\frac{1}{2}
, the premise of
Bounded Rank
is simply the following triple, which is easy to prove using the
Prob
and
Assign
rules.

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0<x\leq 5\land x=n\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~(x\coloneqq x-1)\oplus_{\frac{1}{2}}(x\leftarrow\{1,\ldots,5\})~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}0\leq x<n\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{2}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}0\leq x\leq 5\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}

McIver and Morgan (2005, Lemma 7.6.1)
showed that
Bounded Rank
is complete for proving almost sure termination if the state space is finite. While we did not assume a finite state space for our language, this result nonetheless shows that the rule is broadly applicable. In addition, our new
Bounded Variant
rule is more expressive, as it allows the invariants to have multiple outcomes. We will see an example of how this is useful in
Section

6.2
.

More sophisticated rules are possible in which the rank need not be bounded. One such rule is shown below, based on that of
McIver
et al. (2018)
. Instead of bounding the rank, we now require that the
expected
rank decreases each iteration, which is guaranteed in our rule by bounding the amount that it can increase in the case that an increase occurs.

\hypertarget{rule:Progressing Rank}{\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land e\land e_{\mathsf{rank}}=k\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\land e_{\mathsf{rank}}\leq k-d\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{p}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\land e_{\mathsf{rank}}\leq k+{\textstyle\frac{p}{1-p}}d\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{while}~e~\texttt{do}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\land\lnot e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{\textsc{Progressing Rank}}}

We can use this rule to prove almost sure termination of the following demonically fair random walk, in which the agent steps towards the origin with probability
\frac{1}{2}
, otherwise an adversary can choose whether or not the adversary steps away from the origin.

\begin{array}[]{l}\texttt{while}~x>0~\texttt{do}\\
\quad x\coloneqq x-1\oplus_{\frac{1}{2}}(x\coloneqq x+1\mathbin{\&}\texttt{skip})\end{array}

We instantiate
Progressing Rank
with
P\triangleq x\geq 0
,
e_{\mathsf{rank}}\triangleq x
,
p\triangleq\frac{1}{2}
, and
d\triangleq 1
to get the following premise, which is easily proven using
Prob
,
Nondet
,
Assign
, and basic propositional reasoning.

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=k>0\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x\coloneqq x-1\oplus_{\frac{1}{2}}(x\coloneqq x+1\mathbin{\&}\texttt{skip})~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}0\leq x\leq k-1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{2}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}0\leq x\leq k+1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}

The full derivation for the demonic random walk and soundness proof for a more general version of the
Progressing Rank
rule—where
p
and
d
do not have to be constants—appear in
Section

D.3
.
Compared to the original version of this rule due to
McIver
et al. (2018)
, our rule consolidates three premises into just one.

6.
Case Studies

In this section, we present three case studies in how our logic can be used to analyze programs that contain both probabilistic and nondeterministic operations.

6.1.
The Monty Hall Problem

The Monty Hall problem is a classic paradox in probability theory in which a game show contestant tries to win a car by guessing which door it is behind. The player has three initial choices; the car is behind one door and the other two contain goats. After choosing a door, the host reveals one of the goats among the unopened doors and the player chooses to
stick
with the original door or
switch
—
which strategy is better?

We model this problem with the
Game
program on the left side of
Figure

4
. First, the car is randomly placed behind a door. Next, the player chooses a door. Without loss of generality, we say that the player always chooses door 1. We could have instead universally quantified the choice of the player to indicate that the claim holds for any deterministic strategy. In that case, the proof would be largely the same, although with added cases so that the host does not open the player’s door; we instead fix the player’s choice to be door 1 for simplicity.
Finally, the host
nondeterministically
chooses a door to open, which is neither the player’s pick, nor the car.

\begin{array}[]{ll}\texttt{Game}\triangleq\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{car}\coloneqq 1\oplus_{\frac{1}{3}}(\mathsf{car}\coloneqq 2\oplus_{\frac{1}{2}}\mathsf{car}\coloneqq 3)\fatsemi\\
\mathsf{pick}\coloneqq 1\fatsemi\\
\texttt{if}~\mathsf{car}=1~\texttt{then}\\
\quad\mathsf{open}\leftarrow\{2,3\}\\
\texttt{else if}~\mathsf{car}=2~\texttt{then}\\
\quad\mathsf{open}\coloneqq 3\\
\texttt{else}\\
\quad\mathsf{open}\coloneqq 2\end{array}\right.&\quad\texttt{Switch}\triangleq\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\texttt{if}~\mathsf{open}=2~\texttt{then}\\
\quad\mathsf{pick}\coloneqq 3\\
\texttt{else}\\
\quad\mathsf{pick}\coloneqq 2\end{array}\right.\end{array}

Figure 4
.
Left: the Monty Hall program. Right: additional program to switch doors

We now use Demonic OL to determine the probability of winning (that is,
\mathsf{pick}=\mathsf{car}
) in both the stick strategy (
Game
) and the switch strategy (
\texttt{Game}\fatsemi\texttt{Switch}
, where
Switch
is the program representing the player switching doors, presented on the right of
Figure

4
).
We derive the following triple for the
Game
program:

\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\quad\texttt{Game}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}(\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{car}=1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\land(\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{open}=2\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\mathbin{\&}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{open}=3\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil))\oplus_{\frac{1}{3}}(\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{car}=2\land\mathsf{open}=3\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{2}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{car}=3\land\mathsf{open}=2\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\end{array}

The derivation, using the rules from
Figure

3
, is shown in
Figure

5
. Note that to analyze the if statement, we first use the rule of
Constancy
with
\mathsf{pick}=1
and then de-structure the remaining assertion with two applications of
Prob Split
.
Below, we show manipulation of the postcondition of
Game
to give us the probability of winning using the
stick
strategy.
Irrelevant information about the opened door is first removed. Next, since
\mathsf{pick}=1
in all cases, we weaken
\mathsf{car}=1
to
\mathsf{pick}=\mathsf{car}
, and use
\mathsf{pick}\neq\mathsf{car}
in the other outcomes. Finally, we use idempotence of
\oplus_{\frac{1}{2}}
in the last step.

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{pick}=1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\land((\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{car}=1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\land(\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{open}=2\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\mathbin{\&}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{open}=3\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil))

\displaystyle\qquad\phantom{x}\oplus_{\frac{1}{3}}(\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{car}=2\land\mathsf{open}=3\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{2}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{car}=3\land\mathsf{open}=2\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil))

\displaystyle\quad\implies\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{pick}=1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\land(\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{car}=1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{3}}(\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{car}=2\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{2}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{car}=3\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil))

\displaystyle\quad\implies\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{pick}=\mathsf{car}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{3}}(\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{pick}\neq\mathsf{car}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{2}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{pick}\neq\mathsf{car}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil)

\displaystyle\quad\implies\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{pick}=\mathsf{car}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{3}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{pick}\neq\mathsf{car}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil

So, the player wins with probability
\frac{1}{3}
in the stick strategy.
Now, for the
switch
strategy, we can compositionally reason by appending the
Switch
program to the end of the previous derivation and then continue using the derivation rules. We again must de-structure to analyze the if statement, this time using both
Prob Split
and also
ND Split
.

\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;\texttt{Game}\fatsemi\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}(\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{car}=1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\land(\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{open}=2\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\mathbin{\&}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{open}=3\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil))\oplus_{\frac{1}{3}}(\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{car}=2\land\mathsf{open}=3\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{2}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{car}=3\land\mathsf{open}=2)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;\texttt{if}~\mathsf{open}=2~\texttt{then}~\mathsf{pick}\coloneqq 3~\texttt{else}~\mathsf{pick}\coloneqq 2\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}(\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{car}=1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\land(\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{pick}=3\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\mathbin{\&}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{pick}=2\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil))\oplus_{\frac{1}{3}}(\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{car}=2\land\mathsf{pick}=2\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{2}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{car}=3\land\mathsf{pick}=3\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{pick}\neq\mathsf{car}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{3}}(\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{pick}=\mathsf{car}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{2}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{pick}=\mathsf{car}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{pick}\neq\mathsf{car}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{3}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{pick}=\mathsf{car}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\end{array}

Note that the last two lines of the derivation are obtained by weakening the postcondition with the rule of
Consequence
.
Just like in the previous case, we weaken the postcondition to only assert whether
\mathsf{pick}=\mathsf{car}
or
\mathsf{pick}\neq\mathsf{car}
, and then collapse two outcomes using idempotence of
\oplus_{\frac{1}{2}}
. This time the player wins with probability
\frac{2}{3}
, meaning that switching doors is the better strategy.

\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;\mathsf{car}\coloneqq 1\oplus_{\frac{1}{3}}(\mathsf{car}\coloneqq 2\oplus_{\frac{1}{2}}\mathsf{car}\coloneqq 3)\fatsemi\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{car}=1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{3}}(\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{car}=2\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{2}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{car}=3\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;\mathsf{pick}\coloneqq 1\fatsemi\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{pick}=1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\land(\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{car}=1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{3}}(\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{car}=2\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{2}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{car}=3\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil)){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;\texttt{if}~\mathsf{car}=1~\texttt{then}\\
\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{car}=1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\quad\;\;\mathsf{open}\leftarrow\{2,3\}\\
\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{car}=1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\land(\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{open}=2\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\mathbin{\&}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{open}=3\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;\texttt{else if}~\mathsf{car}=2~\texttt{then}\\
\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{car}=2\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\quad\;\;\mathsf{open}\coloneqq 3\\
\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{car}=2\land\mathsf{open}=3\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;\texttt{else}\\
\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{car}=3\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\quad\;\;\mathsf{open}\coloneqq 2\\
\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{car}=3\land\mathsf{open}=2\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
