\quad\oplus_{\frac{1}{3}}(\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{car}=2\land\mathsf{open}=3\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{2}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{car}=3\land\mathsf{open}=2\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil)\end{array}\right)}\right\rangle}\end{array}

Figure 5
.
Derivation for the
Game
program from
Figure

4
.

6.2.
The Adversarial von Neumann Trick

The
von Neumann (1951)
trick is a protocol for simulating a fair coin using a coin of unknown bias
p
. To do so, the coin is flipped twice. If the outcome is heads, tails—occurring with probability
p(1-p)
—then we consider the result to be heads. If the outcome is tails, heads—which also occurs with probability
p(1-p)
—then we consider to result to be tails. Otherwise, we try again.

In this case study, we work with an
adversarial
version of the von Neumann trick in which an adversary can alter the bias of the coin on each round, as long as the bias is between
\varepsilon
and
1-\varepsilon
for some fixed
0<\varepsilon\leq\frac{1}{2}
. We will show that just like in the original von Neumann trick, and somewhat surprisingly, the simulated coin is fair in the presence of an adversarial bias. To model this protocol, we let the set
[\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon]_{N}
be a finite subset of the interval of rational numbers
[\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon]\subseteq\mathbb{Q}
, formally defined as
[\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon]_{N}\triangleq\{\varepsilon+\frac{k(1-2\varepsilon)}{N}\mid k=0\ldots N\}
. The program is shown below.

\texttt{AdvVonNeumann}\triangleq\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}x\coloneqq\mathsf{false}\fatsemi y\coloneqq\mathsf{false}\fatsemi\\
\texttt{while}~x=y~\texttt{do}\\
\quad p\leftarrow[\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon]_{N}\fatsemi\\
\quad x\coloneqq\mathsf{flip}(p)\fatsemi\\
\quad y\coloneqq\mathsf{flip}(p)\end{array}\right.

So, the program will terminate once
x\neq y
, meaning that one heads and one tails were flipped. We wish to prove that this program almost surely terminates, and that
x=\mathsf{true}
and
x=\mathsf{false}
occur with equal probability, meaning that we have successfully modeled a fair coin. More formally, we will prove that
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\ \texttt{AdvVonNeumann}\ {\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x=\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{2}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x=\mathsf{false}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
.
We will use the
Bounded Variant
rule to analyze the main loop, with the following variants.

\varphi_{0}\triangleq(\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{2}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x=\mathsf{false}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil)\land\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x\neq y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\qquad\qquad\varphi_{1}\triangleq\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x=y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil

The variant
\varphi_{1}
with the higher rank simply states that
x=y
, meaning that the loop will continue to execute. The lower-ranked variant
\varphi_{0}
states both that
x\neq y
—the loop will terminate—and that
x=\mathsf{true}
and
x=\mathsf{false}
both occur with probability
\frac{1}{2}
. This is an example of a variant with multiple outcomes that is not supported in pre-expectation reasoning, as mentioned in
Section

5.3
.

\begin{array}[]{l|l}\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;x\coloneqq\mathsf{false}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lnot x\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;y\coloneqq\mathsf{false}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lnot x\land\lnot y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\implies\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;\texttt{while}~x=y~\texttt{do}\\
\quad\;\;p\leftarrow[\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon]_{N}\fatsemi\\
\quad\;\;x\coloneqq\mathsf{flip}(p)\fatsemi\\
\quad\;\;y\coloneqq\mathsf{flip}(p)\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{2}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x=\mathsf{false}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;p\leftarrow[\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon]_{N}\fatsemi\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\bignd_{q\in[\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon]_{N}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p=q\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;x\coloneqq\mathsf{flip}(p)\fatsemi\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\bignd_{q\in[\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon]_{N}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p=q\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\land(\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x=\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{q}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x=\mathsf{false}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;y\coloneqq\mathsf{flip}(p)\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bignd_{q\in[\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon]_{N}}\begin{array}[]{l}(\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\land(\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x=y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{q}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x\neq y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil))\oplus_{q}\\
(\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=\mathsf{false}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\land(\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x\neq y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{q}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x=y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil))\end{array}}\right\rangle}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\bignd_{q\in[\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon]_{N}}\varphi_{0}\oplus_{2q(1-q)}\varphi_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{0}\oplus_{2\varepsilon(1-\varepsilon)}(\varphi_{0}\mathbin{\&}\varphi_{1}){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 6
.
Derivation of the von Neumann trick program.

Each execution of the loop body will reduce the rank of the variant from 1 to 0 with probability
2p(1-p)
, where
p
is chosen by the adversary. The
worst case
is that the adversary chooses either
p=\varepsilon
or
p=1-\varepsilon
, in which case the probability of terminating the loop is
2\varepsilon(1-\varepsilon)
.
Given that there are only two variants, the
Bounded Variant
rule simplifies to:

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{0}\oplus_{2\varepsilon(1-\varepsilon)}(\varphi_{0}\mathbin{\&}\varphi_{1}){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{while}~e~\texttt{do}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{0}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}

The main derivation is shown on the left of
Figure

6
, and the premise of the
Bounded Variant
rule is on the right. After the two flips, all four probabilistic outcomes are enumerated. This is simplified using the associativity and commutativity rules from
Section

4.1
to conclude that
\varphi_{0}\oplus_{2q(1-q)}\varphi_{1}
for each
q
. As mentioned before, since we know that
2q(1-q)\geq 2\varepsilon(1-\varepsilon)
, we can weaken this to be
\varphi_{0}\oplus_{2\varepsilon(1-\varepsilon)}(\varphi_{0}\mathbin{\&}\varphi_{1})
. Now, since the assertion no longer depends on
q
, we use idempotence to remove the outer
\mathbin{\&}
. In the end, we get the postcondition
\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{2}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x=\mathsf{false}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
, as desired.

6.3.
Probabilistic SAT Solving by Partial Rejection Sampling

Rejection sampling is a standard technique for generating random samples from certain distributions.
A basic version of rejection sampling can be used when a program has a way to generate random samples uniformly from a set
X
, and needs to generate uniform random samples from a set
S
, where
S\subseteq X
.
To do so, a simple rejection sampling procedure will draw a sample
x
from
X
and then check whether
x\in S
.
If
x\in S
, the rejection sampler is said to
accept

x
, and returns it.
However, if
x\notin S
, the sampler is said to
reject

x
, and repeats the process with a fresh sample from
X
.

In some situations, the set
X
is a product of sets
X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{n}
, and a sample
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
from
X
is generated by independently drawing samples
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
, where each
x_{i}\in X_{i}
.
In this case, when
x
is rejected, rather than redrawing
all
of the
x_{i}
to form a new sample from
X
, one might consider instead trying to
partially
resample the components of
x
.
In particular if
x
is
close
to being in
S
, then one might try to only redraw some subset of components
x_{j_{1}},\dots,x_{j_{k}}
, and re-use the other components of
x
to form a new sample
x^{\prime}
to test for membership in
S
.

In general, partial resampling can result in drawing samples that are not
uniformly
distributed over the set
S
.
However,
Guo
et al. (2019)
observed that under certain conditions on the set
S
and
X
, a partial rejection sampling procedure
does
generate uniform samples from
S
.
In particular, when the
x_{i}
are boolean variables, and the test for
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in S
can be encoded as a boolean formula
\varphi
over these variables, then it suffices for
\varphi
to be a so-called
extremal
formula.

Guo
et al. (2019)
showed that many algorithms for sampling combinatorial structures can be formulated in terms of sampling a satisfying assignment to an extremal formula.
In this example, we consider a partial rejection sampler for generating a random satisfying assignment for a formula in 3-CNF form. We will prove that the sampler almost surely terminates if the formula has a satisfying assignment
1
1

1

Since this termination property holds even if the formula does not satisfy the extremal property, we will not formally define the extremal property or assume it as a precondition.
.

\begin{array}[]{l|l|l}\begin{array}[]{l}\texttt{Solve}\triangleq\\
\;\;b\coloneqq\texttt{Eval}\fatsemi\\
\;\;\texttt{while}~\lnot{b}~\texttt{do}\\
\;\;\quad\texttt{SelectClause}\fatsemi\\
\;\;\quad\texttt{SampleClause}\fatsemi\\
\;\;\quad b\coloneqq\texttt{Eval}\\
\\
\texttt{SampleClause}\triangleq\\
\;\;\mathsf{x}[\mathsf{cv}[s][1]]\coloneq\mathsf{flip}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\right)\fatsemi\\
\;\;\mathsf{x}[\mathsf{cv}[s][2]]\coloneq\mathsf{flip}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\right)\fatsemi\\
\;\;\mathsf{x}[\mathsf{cv}[s][3]]\coloneq\mathsf{flip}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\right)\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{l}\texttt{SelectClause}\triangleq\\
\;\;s\coloneq-1\fatsemi\\
\;\;i\coloneq 1\fatsemi\\
\;\;\texttt{while}~i\leq M~\texttt{do}\\
\;\;\quad\texttt{if}~\lnot\texttt{EvalClause}(i)~\texttt{then}\\
\;\;\quad\quad\texttt{if}~s=-1~\texttt{then}\\
\;\;\quad\quad\quad s\coloneq i\\
\;\;\quad\quad\texttt{else}\\
\;\;\quad\quad\quad\texttt{skip}\mathbin{\&}s\coloneqq i\fatsemi\\
\;\;\quad i\coloneq i+1\fatsemi\\
\\
\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{l}\texttt{EvalClause}(i)\triangleq\\
\;\;\left(\mathsf{cs}[i][1]\odot\mathsf{x}[\mathsf{cv}[i][1]]\right)\lor\\
\;\;\left(\mathsf{cs}[i][2]\odot\mathsf{x}[\mathsf{cv}[i][2]]\right)\lor\\
\;\;\left(\mathsf{cs}[i][3]\odot\mathsf{x}[\mathsf{cv}[i][3]]\right)\\
\\
\\
\texttt{Eval}\triangleq\\
\;\;\texttt{EvalClause}(1)\land\\
\;\;\texttt{EvalClause}(2)\land\\
\;\;\qquad\quad\vphantom{\int^{0}}\smash[t]{\vdots}\\
\;\;\texttt{EvalClause}(M)\\
\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 7
.
SAT solving via rejection sampling, split into subroutines.

Figure

7
shows the solver program,
Solve
, broken up into subroutines
2
2

2

Note that our language does not include subroutines, but these routines are interpreted as macros and are inlined into the main program. We only separate them for readability.
.
The clauses are encoded using two 2-dimensional lists,
\mathsf{cv}
and
\mathsf{cs}
, each of size
M\times 3
, where
M
is the number of clauses. See
Section

E.1
for the semantics of list operations. The entry
\mathsf{cv}[i][j]
gives the variable of the
j^{\text{th}}
variable in clause
i
, and
\mathsf{cs}[i][j]
is
0
if this variable occurs in negated form, and is
1
otherwise. The
\odot
operation is
xnor
, so
1\odot 0=0\odot 1=0
and
0\odot 0=1\odot 1=1
. The program stores its current truth-value assignment for each variable in the list
\mathsf{x}
.

Each iteration of the loop in
Solve
starts by nondeterministically selecting an unsatisfied clause
s
to resample via the
SelectClause
subroutine. To do so, it iterates over the clauses, checking if each one is satisfied using
EvalClause
. When an unsatisfied clause is found,
s
is nondeterministically either updated to
i
or left as is (unless
s=-1
, in which case
s
is updated to
i
, to ensure that some unsatisfied clause is picked).
Nondeterminism allows us to under-specify
how
the sampler selects a clause to resample, which in practice might be based on various heuristics. By proving almost-sure termination for this non-deterministic version, we establish almost-sure termination no matter which heuristics are used, including randomized ones.
Presuming that the formula is not yet satisfied (
\texttt{Eval}=\mathsf{false}
), the
SelectClause
routine selects an
s
such that
1\leq s\leq M
and
\texttt{EvalClause}(s)=\mathsf{false}
, which is captured by the following specification and proven in
Appendix

E
.

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\texttt{Eval}=\mathsf{false}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{SelectClause}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}1\leq s\leq M\land\texttt{EvalClause}(s)=\mathsf{false}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}

Next, the three variables in the selected clause are resampled. In order to prove that the program almost surely terminates, we need to show that the resampling operation brings the process closer to termination with nonzero probability. To do this, we measure how close the candidate solution is to some satisfying assignment
\mathsf{x}^{\ast}
(recall we assumed that at least one such satisfying assignment exists). Closeness is measured via the Hamming distance, computed as follows, where the Iverson brackets
[e]
evaluates to 1 if
e
is true and 0 is
e
is false, and
N
is the number of variables.

\mathsf{dist}(x,y)\triangleq\textstyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\big[x[i]\neq y[i]\big]

Now, we can give a specification for
SampleClause
in terms of the Hamming distance. That is, if
\mathsf{dist}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{x}^{\ast})
is initially
k
and clause
s
is not satisfied, then resampling
s
will strictly reduce the Hamming distance with probability at least
\frac{1}{8}
.
The reason for this is that before resampling,
\mathsf{x}
and
\mathsf{x}^{\ast}
must disagree on at least one of the variables in clause
s
, since clause
s
is not satisfied by
\mathsf{x}
.
After resampling, there is at least a
\frac{1}{8}
probability that all 3 resampled variables agree with
\mathsf{x}^{\ast}
, in which case the Hamming distance is reduced by at least 1. The full proof is in
Appendix

E
.

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{dist}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{x}^{\ast})=k\land\texttt{EvalClause}(s)=\mathsf{false}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{SampleClause}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{dist}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{x}^{\ast})<k\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{8}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}

Using these specifications, we now prove that
Solve
almost surely terminates. The derivation is shown in
Figure

8
. We instantiate
Bounded Rank
to analyze the loop with the following parameters:

P\;\;\triangleq\;\;b=\texttt{Eval}\qquad\qquad e_{\mathsf{rank}}\;\;\triangleq\;\;[\lnot\texttt{Eval}]\cdot\mathsf{dist}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{x}^{\ast})\qquad\qquad p\;\;\triangleq\;\;\frac{1}{8}

The invariant
P
simply states that
b
indicates whether the current assignment of variables satisfies the formula. The ranking function is equal to the Hamming distance between
\mathsf{x}
and the sample solution
\mathsf{x}^{\ast}
if the the formula is not yet satisfied, otherwise it is zero, which accounts for the fact that the program may find a solution other than
\mathsf{x}^{\ast}
. We also remark that
e_{\mathsf{rank}}
is bounded between
1
and
N
(where
N
is the total number of variables) as long as the formula is not yet satisfied. As we saw in the specification for
SampleClause
, the probability of reducing the rank is
\frac{1}{8}
.

\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;b\coloneqq\texttt{Eval}\fatsemi\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}b=\texttt{Eval}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;\texttt{while}~\lnot{b}~\texttt{do}\\
\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}b=\texttt{Eval}\land\lnot b\land[\lnot\texttt{Eval}]\cdot\mathsf{dist}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{x}^{\ast})=k\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\texttt{Eval}=\mathsf{false}\land\mathsf{dist}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{x}^{\ast})=k\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;\quad\texttt{SelectClause}\fatsemi\\
\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0\leq s<M\land\texttt{EvalClause}(s)=\mathsf{false}\land\mathsf{dist}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{x}^{\ast})=k\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;\quad\texttt{SampleClause}\fatsemi\\
\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{dist}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{x}^{\ast})<k\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{8}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;\quad b\coloneqq\texttt{Eval}\\
\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}b=\texttt{Eval}\land\mathsf{dist}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{x}^{\ast})<k\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{8}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}b=\texttt{Eval}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}b=\texttt{Eval}\land[\lnot\texttt{Eval}]\cdot\mathsf{dist}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{x}^{\ast})<k\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{8}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}b=\texttt{Eval}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\texttt{Eval}=\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\end{array}

Figure 8
.
Derivation of the
Solve
program, where
\mathsf{x}^{\ast}
is a known satisfying assignment.

Entering the loop, we see that
Eval
must be false, so the rank is just
\mathsf{dist}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{x}^{\ast})
. Applying the specifications for the two subroutines, we prove that the Hamming distance strictly decreases with probability at least
p
. The assignment to
b
then reestablishes the invariant. When the Hamming distance has decreased, we also have that
e_{\mathsf{rank}}
decreased, as multiplying by
[\lnot\texttt{Eval}]
can only make the term smaller. Upon exiting the loop, we have that
b=\mathsf{true}
and hence the final postcondition
\texttt{Eval}=\mathsf{true}
, meaning that the formula is satisfied and the program almost surely terminates.

Aguirre et al. (2024)
prove termination of a similar randomized SAT solving technique using a separation logic for reasoning about upper bounds on probabilities of non-termination. Because the language they consider does not have non-determinism, they fix a particular strategy for selecting clauses to resample, whereas the use of nondeterministic choice in the proof above implies termination for any strategy that selects an unsatisfied clause.
Their proof essentially shows that for any
\varepsilon>0
, after some number of iterations, the Hamming distance will decrease with probability at least
1-\varepsilon
.
The
Bounded Rank
rule effectively encapsulates this kind of reasoning in our proof.

7.
Related Work

Program Logics

Demonic Outcome Logic takes inspiration from program logics for reasoning about purely probabilistic programs, such as Probabilistic Hoare Logic
(
den Hartog, 2002
;
Corin and den
Hartog, 2006
;
den Hartog, 1999
)
, VPHL
(
Rand and
Zdancewic, 2015
)
, Ellora
(
Barthe et al., 2018
)
, and Outcome Logic
(
Zilberstein
et al., 2023
;
Zilberstein
et al., 2024b
;
Zilberstein, 2024
)
.
Those logics provide means to prove properties about the distributions of outcomes in probabilistic programs, to which we added the ability to also reason about demonic nondeterminism.

Although this paper introduces the first logic for reasoning about the
outcomes
in demonic probabilistic programs, there is some prior work on other styles of analysis.
Building on the work of
Varacca (2002)
;
Varacca (2003)
, Polaris is a relational separation logic for reasoning about concurrent probabilistic programs
(
Tassarotti, 2018
;
Tassarotti and
Harper, 2019
)
. Specifications take the form of refinements, where a complex program is shown to behave equivalently to an idealized version. Probabilistic analysis can then be done on the idealized program to determine its expected behavior, but it is external to the program logic. Polaris also does not support unbounded looping, and therefore it cannot be used to analyze our last two case studies.

Weakest Pre-Expectations

Weakest pre-expectation (
\mathsf{wp}
) transformers are calculi for reasoning about probabilistic programs in terms of expected values
(
Morgan
et al., 1996a
)
. They were inspired by propositional weakest precondition calculi
(
Dijkstra, 1975
;
Dijkstra, 1976
)
, Probabilistic Propositional Dynamic Logic
(
Kozen, 1983
)
, and probabilistic predicate transformers
(
Jones, 1990
)
. Refer to
Kaminski (2019)
for a thorough overview of this technique.

From the start,
\mathsf{wp}
supported nondeterminism; in fact,
\mathsf{wp}
emerged from a line of work on semantics for randomized nondeterministic programs
(
He
et al., 1997
;
McIver and Morgan, 2001
;
Morgan
et al., 1996b
)
. Nondeterminism is handled by lower-bounding expectations, corresponding to larger expected values being
better
. An
angelic
variant can alternatively be used for upper bounds.

Work on
\mathsf{wp}
has intersected with termination analysis for probabilistic programs. Some of this work uses martingales
(
Chakarov and
Sankaranarayanan, 2013
)
to show that programs terminate with finite expected running time. More sophisticated techniques exist for almost sure termination too
(
Kaminski, 2019
;
McIver and Morgan, 2005
;
McIver
et al., 2018
)
.

As noted by
Kaminski (2019, §2.3.3)
, the choice of either upper or lower bounding the expected values is
“extremal”
—it forces a view where expectations must be either maximized or minimized, as opposed to our approach where multiple outcomes can be represented in one specification. However, reasoning about outcomes and expectations are not mutually exclusive;
Barthe et al. (2018, Theorem 1)
showed how to embed a
\mathsf{wp}
calculus in a probabilistic program logic. A similar construction is possible in Demonic Outcome Logic.

Powerdomains for Probabilistic Nondeterminism

Powerdomains are a well-studied domain-theoretic tool for reasoning about looping nondeterministic programs, providing a means for defining a continuous domain in which loops can be interpreted as fixed points. This revolves around defining appropriate orders over sets of states to show that iterated actions eventually converge.
Given a partially ordered domain of program states
\langle\Sigma,\leq\rangle
, there are three typical choices for orders over sets of states, known as the Hoare,
Smyth (1978)
, and Egli-Milner orders, defined below:

\begin{array}[]{lclcl}S&\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{H}}&T&\qquad\text{iff}&\forall\sigma\in S.\quad\exists\tau\in T.\quad\sigma\leq\tau\\
S&\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{S}}&T&\text{iff}&\forall\tau\in T.\quad\exists\sigma\in S.\quad\sigma\leq\tau\\
S&\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{EM}}&T&\text{iff}&S\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{H}}T\quad\text{and}\quad S\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{S}}T\end{array}

In general, none of these relations are antisymmetric, making them
preorders
, whereas domain theoretic tools for finding fixed points operate on
partial orders
. So the sets representing the program semantics must be closed in order to obtain a proper domain. This closure operation loses precision of the semantics, incorporating additional possibilities which are not always intuitive.

The Hoare order requires a down-closure, essentially meaning that nontermination may always be an option. This makes it a good choice for partial correctness, where we only wish to determine what happens
if
the program terminates, as in Hoare Logic.
The
Smyth (1978)
order, which we use in this paper, requires an upwards closure, so that nontermination becomes erratic behavior. This makes it a good choice for total correctness
(
Manna and Pnueli, 1974
)
, which is concerned only with terminating programs where erratic behavior does not arise.

In the Egli-Milner case—and the associated
Plotkin
Powerdomain [
(
1976
)
]—the more precise, but also less intuitive Egli-Milner closure is used.

McIver and Morgan (2001)
created a denotational model where fixed points are taken with respect to the Egli-Milner order rather than the
Smyth (1978)
one.
As such, they require the domain of computation to be Egli-Milner closed, which means that
S=\mathord{\uparrow}S\cap\mathord{\downarrow}S
. Unlike up-closedness (required for the Smyth approach), which is preserved by all the operations in
Figure

1
, the semantics of
McIver and Morgan (2001)
must take the Egli-Milner closure after nondeterministic and probabilistic choice and after sequential composition, making the model more complex and adding outcomes that do not have an obvious operational meaning. Refer to
Tix
et al. (2009)
;
Keimel and
Plotkin (2017)
for a more complete exploration of that approach.

Let us examine the semantics of a coin flip in order to demonstrate why the Smyth order is preferable to Hoare. The variable
x
is assigned the values
\mathsf{true}
or
\mathsf{false}
each with probability
\frac{1}{2}
. So, the result of running the program is a singleton set containing the aforementioned distribution.

\left\llbracket x\coloneqq\mathsf{flip}\left({\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\right)\right\rrbracket(\sigma)=\left\{\;\;\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{llcl}\sigma[x\coloneqq\mathsf{true}&]&\hskip 8.19447pt\mapsto&\frac{1}{2}\\
\sigma[x\coloneqq\mathsf{false}&]&\mapsto&\frac{1}{2}\end{array}\;\;\right\}

If we were to use the Hoare powerdomain, then we would need to down-close this set, adding all smaller distributions too. This not only means that nontermination is possible, but we would not even be able to determine that
x=\mathsf{true}
and
x=\mathsf{false}
occur with equal probability.

\left\llbracket x\coloneqq\mathsf{flip}\left({\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\right)\right\rrbracket(\sigma)=\left\{\;\;\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{llcl}\sigma[x\coloneqq\mathsf{true}&]&\hskip 8.19447pt\mapsto&p\\
\sigma[x\coloneqq\mathsf{false}&]&\mapsto&q\\
\bot&&\mapsto&1-p-q\end{array}\;\;\middle|\hskip 8.19447ptp\leq\frac{1}{2},~q\leq\frac{1}{2}\hskip 8.19447pt\right\}

This was the approach taken by
Varacca (2002)
, and the loss of precision is reflected in the adequacy theorems of that work. In particular,
Varacca
’s Proposition 6.10 shows that the denotational model includes outcomes that may not be possible according to the associated operational model.
By contrast, the up-closure—required by the Smyth order—adds nothing for this program; the semantics is already a full distribution and therefore there are no distributions larger than it. We can therefore conclude that the two outcomes occur with probability exactly
\frac{1}{2}
, as desired.

This example demonstrates that the notion of partial correctness (as embodied by the Hoare order) does not make much sense in probabilistic settings, since it translates to uncertainty about the minimum probability of an event. Total correctness, on the other hand, does make sense, and corresponds to the notion of
almost sure termination
, which is a property of great interest in probabilistic program analysis
(
McIver
et al., 2018
;
Chakarov and
Sankaranarayanan, 2013
)
.

The problem with the Smyth order is that a semantics based on it is not
Scott (1972)
continuous in the presence of unbounded nondeterminism
(
Apt and Plotkin, 1986
;
Søndergaard and
Sestoft, 1992
)
. This is the reason why
He
et al. (1997)
instead use the Knaster-Tarski theorem to guarantee the fixed point existence via transfinite iteration, which only requires monotonicity and not Scott continuity. The main shortcoming of
He
et al.
’s approach is that it did not guarantee non-emptiness of the set of result distributions, meaning that some programs may have vacuous semantics.

To address this,
Morgan
et al. (1996a)
added the additional requirement that domain only include topologically closed sets (
Morgan
et al.
called this property
Cauchy closure
).
As we mentioned in
Section

3.3
and proved in
Section

B.3
, closure ensures that no programs are modeled as empty sets, but it also prevents commands from exhibiting unbounded nondeterminism. For example, it is not possible to represent a program
x\coloneqq\bigstar
, which nondeterministically selects a value for
x
from the natural numbers—the set
\mathbb{N}
is not closed since it does not contain a limit point.

McIver and Morgan (2005)
suggested that topological closure opens up the possibility of Scott continuity.
In addition, there has been work to combine classical powerdomains for nondeterminism
(
Plotkin, 1976
;
Smyth, 1978
)
with the probabilistic powerdomain of
Jones (1990)
;
Jones and Plotkin (1989)
. This was first pursued by
Tix (1999)
, and was later refined in
Tix (2000)
;
Tix
et al. (2009)
;
Keimel and
Plotkin (2017)
. They obtain a Scott continuous composition operation (which they call
\widehat{f}
) via a universal property, as opposed to the direct construction of
Jacobs (2008)
that we use.

Monads for Probabilistic Nondeterminism

Varacca (2002)
;
Varacca (2003)
introduced powersets of
indexed valuations
. An indexed valuation behaves similarly to a distribution, but the idempotence property is removed, so that
X\oplus_{p}X\neq X
.
As shown by
Varacca and
Winskel (2006)
, a powerset of indexed valuations has a
Beck (1969)
distributive law, and is therefore a monad. However, indexed valuations are difficult to work with since equivalence is taken modulo renaming of the indices.

Varacca (2002, Theorem 6.5)
proved that denotational models based on indexed valuations are equivalent to operational models in which a
deterministic
scheduler resolves the nondeterminism. Given our goals of modeling
adversarial
nondeterminism, we opted to use convex sets, which model a more powerful probabilistic scheduler, giving us robust guarantees in a stronger threat model.

An alternative approach is to flip the order of composition and work instead with distributions of nondeterministic outcomes. While the distribution monad does not compose with powerset, it does compose with multiset, as shown by
Jacobs (2021)
and further explored by
Kozen and Silva (2024)
.
The barrier to this approach is that the multisets must be finite, but it is easy to construct programs that reach infinitely many nondeterministic outcomes via while loops. So this model cannot be used to represent arbitrary programs from the language in
Section

3
.
The use of multiset instead of powerset is again an instance of removing an idempotence law, this time for nondeterminism:
X\mathbin{\&}X\neq X
. Indeed, idempotence is the key reason why no distributive law exists in both cases
(
Parlant, 2020
;
Zwart and Marsden, 2019
;
Zwart, 2020
)
.

Other Semantic Approaches

Segala (1995)
created a model in which a tree of alternating probabilistic and nondeterministic choices is collapsed into a set of distributions collected from all combinations of nondeterministic choices. However, this model does not lead to a compositional semantics.
Additional operational models of probabilistic nondeterminism have been studied through the lens of process algebras
(
den Hartog, 2002
;
den Hartog and
de Vink, 1999
;
den Hartog, 1998
;
Mislove
et al., 2004
)
.
In addition, coalgebraic methods have been used to define trace semantics and establish bisimilarity of randomized nondeterministic automata
(
Jacobs, 2008
;
Bonchi
et al., 2019
;
Bonchi
et al., 2021b
;
Bonchi
et al., 2022
;
Bonchi
et al., 2021a
)
.

Aguirre and
Birkedal (2023)
note the difficulties of building denotational models that combine probabilistic and nondeterministic choice with other challenging semantic features. Instead, they start with an operational semantics for probabilistic and nondeterministic choice and then construct a step-indexed logical relations model for a typed, higher-order language with polymorphism and recursive types. Using this logical relations model, they derive an equational theory for contextual equivalence and show that it validates many of the equations found in denotational models.

8.
Conclusion

This paper introduced
Demonic Outcome Logic
, a logic for outcome based reasoning about programs that are both randomized and nondeterministic, a combination that presents many challenges for program semantics and analysis. The logic includes several novel features, such as equational laws for manipulating pre- and postconditions and rules for loops that both establish termination and quantify the distribution of final outcomes from a single premise. We build on a large body of work on semantics for probabilistic nondeterminism
(
He
et al., 1997
;
Morgan
et al., 1996b
;
Morgan
et al., 1996a
;
Jacobs, 2008
;
Tix
et al., 2009
;
Varacca, 2002
)
, and also draw inspiration from Outcome Logic
(
Zilberstein
et al., 2023
;
Zilberstein
et al., 2024b
;
Zilberstein, 2024
)
and weakest pre-expectation calculi
(
Morgan
et al., 1996a
;
Kaminski, 2019
;
Zhang
et al., 2024
)
. The resulting logic contains rules that enable effective reasoning about distributions of outcomes in randomized nondeterministic programs, as illustrated through the three presented case studies. The simplicity of the rules is enabled by a carefully chosen denotational semantics that allows us to hide the complex algebraic properties of the domain in the proof of their soundness. Compared to weakest pre-expectation reasoning, the propositional approach afforded by Demonic Outcome Logic enables reasoning about multiple outcomes in tandem, leading to more expressive specifications, and the loop rules rely on fewer, simpler premises.

Moving forward, we want to go beyond standard nondeterminism and extend the logic for reasoning about probabilistic fine-grain concurrency with shared memory. This will require fundamental changes to the denotational semantics and inference rules, although prior work on Concurrent Separation Logic
(
O’Hearn, 2004
)
, Outcome Separation Logic
(
Zilberstein
et al., 2024b
)
, and Concurrent Kleene Algebra
(
Hoare et al., 2011
)
will provide a good source of inspiration.
Using the resulting logic, we will verify concurrent algorithms such as distributed cryptographic protocols, for which state of the art techniques use limited models of concurrency and operate by establishing observational equivalence and then separately proving properties of an idealized program
(
Gancher et al., 2023
)
. By contrast, we plan to develop a logic based on a fine-grain concurrency model, which can prove direct specifications involving probabilistic outcomes. We also plan to explore a mechanized implementation of the logic, building on existing frameworks for (concurrent) separation logic such as Iris
(
Jung et al., 2015
)
.

Appendix

Appendix A
Examples and Counterexamples

A.1.
Non-Idempotence of Logical Operators

In this section, we show why the idempotence rule
\varphi\mathbin{\&}\varphi\Rightarrow\varphi
does not apply when assertions model sets of distributions of states. To do so, we first need to define a new semantics for the logical operators:

\begin{array}[]{lcllllll}S\vDash\varphi\mathbin{\&}\psi&\text{iff}&S=S_{1}\mathbin{\&}S_{2}&\text{and}&S_{1}\vDash\varphi&\text{and}&S_{2}\vDash\psi&\text{for some}~S_{1},S_{2}\in\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)\\
S\vDash\varphi\oplus_{p}\psi&\text{iff}&S=S_{1}\oplus_{p}S_{2}&\text{and}&S_{1}\vDash\varphi&\text{and}&S_{2}\vDash\psi&\text{for some}~S_{1},S_{2}\in\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)\\
S\vDash\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil&\text{iff}&\lx@intercol\bigcup_{\mu\in S}\mathsf{supp}(\mu)\subseteq\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}

This is similar to how the
outcome conjunction
of Outcome Logic is defined, where the collection of outcomes is split according to the same nondeterminism operation as is used in the program semantics, to satisfy the two assertions,
\varphi
and
\psi
, individually
(
Zilberstein
et al., 2023
)
.
Now, let us revisit the coin flip game. The semantics of the program is shown below.

\left\llbracket y\leftarrow\mathbb{B}\fatsemi x\coloneqq\mathsf{flip}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\right)\right\rrbracket(\sigma)=\left\{\;\begin{array}[]{lll}\sigma[x\coloneqq\mathsf{true},&y\coloneqq\mathsf{true}&]\mapsto\frac{1}{2}\cdot p\\
\sigma[x\coloneqq\mathsf{false},&y\coloneqq\mathsf{true}&]\mapsto\frac{1}{2}\cdot p\\
\sigma[x\coloneqq\mathsf{true},&y\coloneqq\mathsf{false}&]\mapsto\frac{1}{2}\cdot(1-p)\\
\sigma[x\coloneqq\mathsf{false},&y\coloneqq\mathsf{false}&]\mapsto\frac{1}{2}\cdot(1-p)\end{array}\quad\middle|\quad p\in[0,1]~\right\}

Let us call this set
S
.
It is not hard to see that
S
is the convex union of two sets that satisfy
\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{2}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x\neq y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
, so we have that:

S\vDash(\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{2}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x\neq y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil)\mathbin{\&}(\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x=y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{2}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x\neq y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil)

It is tempting to say that
S\vDash\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{2}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x\neq y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
, but this is not the case. To prove this, we will show that
S\neq S_{1}\mathbin{\&}S_{2}
for any
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
such that
S_{1}\vDash\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
and
S_{2}\vDash\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x\neq y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
. If
S_{1}\vDash\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
and
S_{2}\vDash\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x\neq y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
, then they must be of the following forms, where
T_{1},T_{2}\subseteq[0,1]
.

\footnotesize S_{1}\triangleq\left\{\;\begin{array}[]{lll}\sigma[x\coloneqq\mathsf{true},&y\coloneqq\mathsf{true}&]\mapsto p\\
\sigma[x\coloneqq\mathsf{false},&y\coloneqq\mathsf{false}&]\mapsto 1-p\end{array}\;\middle|\;p\in T_{1}\;\right\}\hskip 17.00024ptS_{2}\triangleq\left\{\;\begin{array}[]{lll}\sigma[x\coloneqq\mathsf{true},&y\coloneqq\mathsf{false}&]\mapsto q\\
\sigma[x\coloneqq\mathsf{false},&y\coloneqq\mathsf{true}&]\mapsto 1-q\end{array}\;\middle|\;q\in T_{2}\;\right\}

Clearly,
S_{1}\vDash\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
and
S_{2}\vDash\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x\neq y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
, but
S\neq S_{1}\mathbin{\&}S_{2}
. To see this, let us consider
S_{1}\mathbin{\&}S_{2}
:

\displaystyle S_{1}\mathbin{\&}S_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\{\;\begin{array}[]{lll}\sigma[x\coloneqq\mathsf{true},&y\coloneqq\mathsf{true}&]\mapsto r\cdot p\\
\sigma[x\coloneqq\mathsf{false},&y\coloneqq\mathsf{false}&]\mapsto r\cdot(1-p)\\
\sigma[x\coloneqq\mathsf{true},&y\coloneqq\mathsf{false}&]\mapsto(1-r)\cdot q\\
\sigma[x\coloneqq\mathsf{false},&y\coloneqq\mathsf{true}&]\mapsto(1-r)\cdot(1-q)\end{array}\;\middle|\;\;r\in[0,1],~p\in T_{1},~q\in T_{2}\;\;\right\}

Whereas in
S
, the probability that
x
is true or false given a fixed value of
y
is always exactly
\frac{1}{2}
, that is not the case in
S_{1}\mathbin{\&}S_{2}
. For instance, when
r=1
and
p
is any arbitrary element of
T_{1}
, we get:

\left(\begin{array}[]{lll}\sigma[x\coloneqq\mathsf{true},&y\coloneqq\mathsf{true}&]\mapsto p\\
\sigma[x\coloneqq\mathsf{false},&y\coloneqq\mathsf{false}&]\mapsto 1-p\end{array}\right)\in S_{1}\mathbin{\&}S_{2}

But clearly that distribution is not in
S
, since the two outcomes where
x=y
must occur with aggregate probability
\frac{1}{2}
.
So, we just saw that for any
S_{1}\vDash\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
and
S_{2}\vDash\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x\neq y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
, there exists a
\mu\in S_{1}\mathbin{\&}S_{2}
such that
\mu\notin S
. This suggests we could modifiy the semantics above to say that
S\subseteq S_{1}\mathbin{\&}S_{2}
instead of
S=S_{1}\mathbin{\&}S_{2}
:

S\vDash\varphi\mathbin{\&}\psi\quad\text{iff}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}S\subseteq S_{1}\mathbin{\&}S_{2}}\quad\text{and}\quad S_{1}\vDash\varphi\quad\text{and}\quad S_{2}\vDash\psi\quad\text{for some}~S_{1},S_{2}\in\mathcal{C}(\Sigma_{\bot})

However, this removes all reachability claims since
e.g.,

S_{1}\subseteq S_{1}\mathbin{\&}S_{2}
, and therefore
\varphi\Rightarrow\varphi\mathbin{\&}\psi
for any satisfiable
\psi
. In fact, we get that
S\vDash\varphi\mathbin{\&}\psi
iff for every
\mu\in S
, there exists a probability
p
such that
\mu\vDash\varphi\oplus_{p}\psi
, which is exactly the semantics we have defined in
Section

4.1
. This suggests that a demonic interpretation is inevitable if idempotence is a desired propositional property.

A.2.

In this section, we give the full derivation for the coin flip game that was introduced in
Section

2
. Below, we show the derivation for the variant in which the adversary picks first. The variant in which the coin flip happens first is completely analogous.

(5)

\frac{\frac{\;}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{true}=y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x\coloneqq\mathsf{true}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x=y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\quad\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Assign}{{Assign}}}\quad\frac{\frac{\;}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{false}\neq y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x\coloneqq\mathsf{false}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x\neq y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{Right=\hyperlink{rule:Assign}{{Assign}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{true}=y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x\coloneqq\mathsf{false}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x\neq y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\quad\textsc{Right=\hyperlink{rule:Consequence}{{Consequence}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{true}=y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x\coloneqq\mathsf{flip}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\right)~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x=y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{2}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x\neq y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\quad\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Prob}{{Prob}}}

Appendix B
Semantics

In this section, we provide some of the details about the well-definedness of the program semantics that are omitted from
Section

3
.

B.1.
Properties of the Kleisli Extension

Lemma B.1.

If
S\mathrel{\subseteq}\mathcal{D}(X)
is convex and closed, then
S
is countably convex; that is, every countable convex combination of elements of
S
is in
S
.

Proof.

The basic open sets are of the form
(\prod_{x\in F}U_{x})\times[0,1]^{X\setminus F}
, where
F\mathrel{\subseteq}X
is finite and
U_{x}
are basic open intervals. Let
\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}\mu_{i}
be a countable convex combination of elements of
S
,
a_{0}>0
. Let
c_{n}=\sum_{i\leq n}a_{i}
. By convexity,
\sum_{i\leq n}(a_{i}/c_{n})\mu_{i}
are in
S
, since these are finite convex combinations. Since
c_{n}\to 1
, for sufficiently large
n
,
c_{n}>a_{i}/(\varepsilon+a_{i})
, so
(a_{i}/c_{n})-a_{i}<\varepsilon
. Thus every basic open set
(\prod_{x\in F}U_{x})\times[0,1]^{X\setminus F}
containing
\sum_{i}a_{i}\mu_{i}
contains all but finitely many
\sum_{i\leq n}(a_{i}/c_{n})\mu_{i}
. Since
S
is closed,
\sum_{i}a_{i}\mu_{i}\in S
.
∎

In
Section

3.1
, we introduced the Kleisi extension
f^{\dagger}\colon\mathcal{C}(X)\to\mathcal{C}(Y)
, but to verify this typing we must show that
f^{\dagger}(S)\in\mathcal{C}(Y)
for any
f\colon X\to\mathcal{C}(Y)
and
S\in\mathcal{C}(X)
, that is,
f^{\dagger}(S)
is convex, closed, and up-closed. It was asserted by
He
et al. (1997)
that
f^{\dagger}(S)
is convex and up-closed, but they did not present the proof, so we provide it here.

Lemma B.2.

f^{\dagger}(S)
is convex.

Proof.

Take any
\mu,\nu\in f^{\dagger}(S)
, so that means that there must be
\mu^{\prime},\nu^{\prime}\in S
,
(\mu_{x})_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu^{\prime})}
, and
(\nu_{x})_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(\nu^{\prime})}
such that
\mu=\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu^{\prime})}\mu^{\prime}(x)\mu_{x}
and
\nu=\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(\nu^{\prime})}\nu^{\prime}(x)\nu_{x}
and
\mu_{x},\nu_{x}\in f(x)
for all
x
. Now, take any
p
:

\displaystyle p\mu+(1-p)\nu

\displaystyle=p\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu^{\prime})}\mu^{\prime}(x)\mu_{x}+(1-p)\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(\nu^{\prime})}\nu^{\prime}(x)\nu_{x}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in X}p\mu^{\prime}(x)\mu_{x}+(1-p)\nu^{\prime}(x)\nu_{x}

Let
\xi=p\mu^{\prime}+(1-p)\nu^{\prime}
. Clearly
\xi\in S
since it is a convex combination of
\mu^{\prime},\nu^{\prime}\in S
.

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(\xi)}\xi(x)\left(p\frac{\mu^{\prime}(x)}{\xi(x)}\mu_{x}+(1-p)\frac{\nu^{\prime}(x)}{\xi(x)}\nu_{x}\right)

Now, we also clearly see that
p\frac{\mu^{\prime}(x)}{\xi(x)}\mu_{x}+(1-p)\frac{\nu^{\prime}(x)}{\xi(x)}\nu_{x}\in f(x)
since it is a convex combination of
\mu_{x}
and
\nu_{x}
. Therefore,
p\mu+(1-p)\nu\in f^{\dagger}(S)
, and therefore
f^{\dagger}(S)
is convex.

∎

Lemma B.3.

f^{\dagger}(S)
is closed in the product topology.

Proof.

Let

\displaystyle h\colon\mathcal{D}(X)\times\prod_{x\in X}\mathcal{D}(Y_{\bot})\to\mathcal{D}(Y_{\bot})

\displaystyle h(\mu,(\nu_{x})_{x\in X})=\sum_{x\in X}\mu(x)\nu_{x}

We first show that
h
is continuous in the product topology. We must to show that the preimage of any subbasic open set
(a,b)\times[0,1]^{Y_{\bot}\setminus y}
of
[0,1]^{Y_{\bot}}
is open in
\mathcal{D}(X)\times\prod_{x\in X}\mathcal{D}(Y_{\bot})
. The preimage is

\displaystyle h^{-1}((a,b)\times[0,1]^{Y_{\bot}\setminus y})

\displaystyle=\left\{(\mu,(\nu_{x})_{x\in X})\;\;\middle|\;\;h(\mu,(\nu_{x})_{x\in X})\in(a,b)\times[0,1]^{Y_{\bot}\setminus y}\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{(\mu,(\nu_{x})_{x\in X})\;\;\middle|\;\;\sum_{x\in X}\mu(x)\nu_{x}\in(a,b)\times[0,1]^{Y_{\bot}\setminus y}\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{(\mu,(\nu_{x})_{x\in X})\;\;\middle|\;\;\sum_{x\in X}\mu(x)\nu_{x}(y)\in(a,b)\right\}.

If this set is nonempty, say
\sum_{x\in X}c(x)\mu_{x}(y)\in(a,b)
, let
0<\varepsilon<(b-a)/2
and let
F\mathrel{\subseteq}X
be a large enough finite set that
\sum_{x\in F}c(x)\mu_{x}(y)\in(a+\varepsilon,b-\varepsilon)
. Let
\delta>0
be such that
2\delta+\delta^{2}<\varepsilon/|F|
and define

\displaystyle U

\displaystyle=\left\{\mu\in\mathcal{D}(X)\;\;\middle|\;\;\forall x\in F\ \mu(x)\in(c(x)-\delta,c(x)+\delta)\right\}

\displaystyle V_{x}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\left\{\nu\in f(x)\;\;\middle|\;\;\nu_{x}(y)\in(\mu_{x}(y)-\delta,\mu_{x}(y)+\delta)\right\},&\text{if $x\in F$,}\\
f(x),&\text{if $x\in X\setminus F$.}\end{cases}

Then
U\times\prod_{x\in F}V_{x}
is an open subset of
\mathcal{D}(X)\times\prod_{x\in X}f(x)
in the relative topology. If
(d,(\rho_{x})_{x\in X})\in U\times\prod_{x\in X}V_{x}
, then for all
x\in F
,

\displaystyle c_{x}-\delta<d(x)<c(x)+\delta

\displaystyle\mu_{x}(y)-\delta<\rho_{x}(y)<\mu_{x}(y)+\delta.

It follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in F}(c(x)-\delta)(\mu_{x}(y)-\delta)<\sum_{x\in F}d(x)\rho_{x}(y)<\sum_{x\in F}(c(x)+\delta)(\mu_{x}(y)+\delta)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\ \sum_{x\in F}c(x)\mu_{x}(y)-(2\delta-\delta^{2})|F|<\sum_{x\in F}d(x)\rho_{x}(y)<\sum_{x\in F}c(x)\mu_{x}(y)+(2\delta+\delta^{2})|F|

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\ \sum_{x\in F}c(x)\mu_{x}(y)-\varepsilon<\sum_{x\in F}d(x)\rho_{x}(y)<\sum_{x\in F}c(x)\mu_{x}(y)+\varepsilon

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\ \sum_{x\in F}d(x)\rho_{x}(y)\in(a,b),

therefore

\displaystyle U\times\prod_{x\in X}V_{x}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\subseteq}\left\{(\mu,\nu_{x}\mid x\in X)\;\;\middle|\;\;\sum_{x\in X}\mu(x)\nu_{x}(y)\in(a,b)\right\}.

We have shown that
h
is continuous. The set
S\times\prod_{x\in X}f(x)
is a closed subset of
\mathcal{D}(X)\times\prod_{x\in X}\mathcal{D}(Y_{\bot})
and its image under
h
is
f^{\dagger}(S)
. The space
\mathcal{D}(X)\times\prod_{x\in X}\mathcal{D}(Y_{\bot})
, being a closed subset of the compact space
[0,1]^{X}\times\prod_{x\in X}[0,1]^{Y_{\bot}}
, is itself compact in the relative topology. The space
[0,1]^{Y_{\bot}}
is clearly Hausdorff, and it is well known that the image of a closed set under a continuous map from a compact space to a Hausdorff space is closed.
∎

Lemma B.4.

f^{\dagger}(S)
is up-closed.

Proof.

Take any
\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(x)\nu_{x}\in S
and let
\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(x)\nu_{x}\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{D}}\xi
, so for all
y\in Y
:

\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(x)\cdotp\nu_{x}(y)\leq\xi(y)

Let
\left\{a_{x}\;\;\middle|\;\;x\in X\right\}
be a collection of probability distributions on
Y_{\bot}
such that the
a_{x}
are
\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{D}}
-maximal subject to

\triangleright

\forall x\in X\ \ a_{x}(Y)\leq\nu_{x}(\bot)

\triangleright

\forall s\in Y\ \ \sum_{x}b_{x}a_{x}(s)\leq(\mu-\sum_{x}b_{x}\nu_{x})(s)
.

The distributions
a_{x}=\delta_{\bot}
satisfy these constraints, so there exists a maximal one.
By maximality, one of the two constraints must be universally tight, that is, either

\triangleright

\forall x\in X\ \ a_{x}(Y)=\nu_{x}(\bot)
, or

\triangleright

\forall s\in Y\ \ \sum_{x}b_{x}a_{x}(s)=(\mu-\sum_{x}b_{x}\nu_{x})(s)
.

Let
\nu_{x}^{\prime}(s)=\nu_{x}(s)+a_{x}(s)
for
s\in Y
and
\nu_{x}^{\prime}(\bot)=\nu_{x}(\bot)-a_{x}(Y)
. For all
s\in Y
,

\displaystyle\sum_{x}b_{x}a_{x}(s)\leq(\mu-\sum_{x}b_{x}\nu_{x})(s)\

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\ \sum_{x}b_{x}\nu_{x}(s)+\sum_{x}b_{x}a_{x}(s)\leq\mu(s)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\ \sum_{x}b_{x}\nu_{x}^{\prime}(s)\leq\mu(s),

so
\sum_{x}b_{x}\nu_{x}^{\prime}\sqsubseteq\mu
.
If the first constraint is universally tight, then all
\nu_{x}^{\prime}(\bot)=0
, thus
\mu(\bot)\leq\sum_{x}b_{x}\nu_{x}^{\prime}(\bot)=0
. Since
\sum_{x}b_{x}\nu_{x}^{\prime}\sqsubseteq\mu
and
\mu(\bot)=\sum_{x}b_{x}\nu_{x}^{\prime}(\bot)=0
, we have
\sum_{x}b_{x}\nu_{x}^{\prime}=\mu
, so the second constraint is universally tight as well. Thus in either case,
\sum_{x}b_{x}\nu_{x}^{\prime}=\mu
.

As
f(x)
is up-closed and
\nu_{x}\sqsubseteq\nu_{x}^{\prime}
, we have
\nu_{x}^{\prime}\in f(x)
, thus
\mu=\sum_{x}b_{x}\nu_{x}^{\prime}\in f^{\dagger}(S)
by definition of
f^{\dagger}
. Since
\mu\sqsupseteq\sum_{x}b_{x}\nu_{x}
was arbitrary,
f^{\dagger}(S)
is up-closed.
∎

B.2.
Monad Laws

Theorem B.5 (Left Identity).

\eta^{\dagger}=\mathsf{id}

Proof.

For any
S\in\mathcal{C}(X)
:

\displaystyle\eta^{\dagger}(S)

\displaystyle=\left\{\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(x)\cdotp\nu_{x}\;\;\middle|\;\;\mu\in S,\forall x\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu).\nu_{x}\in\eta_{\bot}(x)\right\}

Note that
\eta_{\bot}(x)=\eta(x)=\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\delta_{x}\}}
.

\displaystyle=\left\{\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(x)\cdotp\nu_{x}\;\;\middle|\;\;\mu\in S,\forall x\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu).\nu_{x}\in\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\delta_{x}\}}\right\}

The set above contains every
\mu\in S
, as well as every
\mu^{\prime}
such that
\mu\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{D}}\mu^{\prime}
for some
\mu\in S
. In other words, the set above is
\mathord{\uparrow}{S}
, however,
S
is already up-closed, so the set above is simply
S
.

\displaystyle=\mathord{\uparrow}{S}=S

∎

Theorem B.6 (Right Identity).

f^{\dagger}\circ\eta=f

Proof.

For any
f\colon X\to\mathcal{C}(Y)
and
x\in X
:

\displaystyle f^{\dagger}(\eta(x))

\displaystyle=\left\{\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(x)\cdotp\nu_{x}\;\;\middle|\;\;\mu\in\eta(x),\forall x\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu).\nu_{x}\in f_{\bot}(x)\right\}

Since
x\neq\bot
, then
\eta(x)=\{\delta_{x}\}
and
f_{\bot}(x)=f(x)
.

\displaystyle=\{\nu_{x}\mid\nu_{x}\in f_{\bot}(x)\}=f(x)

∎

Lemma B.7.

For any
\mu\in\mathcal{D}(X)
, collection
(\nu_{x})_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}
such that each
\nu_{x}\in\mathcal{D}(Y)
, and collection
(S_{y})_{y\in Y}
such that each
S_{y}\in\mathcal{C}(Z)
:

\left\{\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(x)\cdot\sum_{y\in\mathsf{supp}(\nu_{x})}\nu_{x}(y)\cdotp\nu^{\prime}_{x,y}~\Big|~\forall x,y.\ \nu^{\prime}_{x,y}\in S_{y}\right\}=\left\{{\sum_{y\in Y}}\left(\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(x)\cdotp\nu_{x}\right)(y)\cdotp\nu^{\prime}_{y}~\Big|~\forall x,y.\ \nu^{\prime}_{y}\in S_{y}\right\}

Proof.

We prove the equality by establishing that each set is a subset of the other. We first prove the
\supseteq
direction. Any element
\xi
from the set on the left has the form
\sum_{x}\mu(x)\cdotp\sum_{y}\nu_{x}(y)\cdotp\nu^{\prime}_{x,y}
where
\nu^{\prime}_{x,y}\in S_{y}
for each
x
and
y
. Now, for each
y\in Y
, we construct
\xi_{y}
as follows:

\xi_{y}\triangleq\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\frac{\mu(x)\cdotp\nu_{x}(y)}{\sum_{x^{\prime}\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(x^{\prime})\cdotp\nu_{x^{\prime}}(y)}\cdotp\nu^{\prime}_{x,y}

Since each
\nu^{\prime}_{x,y}\in S_{y}
, then clearly
\xi_{y}
is a convex combination of elements of
S_{y}
, and since
S_{y}
is convex, then
\xi_{y}\in S_{y}
for each
y
. Now, we have:

\displaystyle\xi

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(x)\cdot\sum_{y\in\mathsf{supp}(\nu_{x})}\nu_{x}(y)\cdot\nu^{\prime}_{x,y}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in Y}\cancel{\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(x)\cdot\nu_{x}(y)}\cdot\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\frac{\mu(x)\cdotp\nu_{x}(y)}{\cancel{\sum_{x^{\prime}\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(x^{\prime})\cdotp\nu_{x^{\prime}}(y)}}\cdotp\nu^{\prime}_{x,y}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in Y}\left(\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(x)\cdot\nu_{x}\right)(y)\cdot\xi_{y}

Therefore
\xi
is contained in the second set too. Now, we prove the
\supseteq
direction. Any element
\xi
of the second set has the form
\xi=\sum_{y}(\sum_{x}\mu(x)\cdotp\nu_{x})(y)\cdotp\nu^{\prime}_{y}=\sum_{x}\mu(x)\cdotp\sum_{y}\nu_{x}(y)\cdotp\nu^{\prime}_{y}
where
\nu^{\prime}_{y}\in S_{y}
for each
y
. Now, for each
x
and
y
, let
\xi_{x,y}=\nu^{\prime}_{y}
, which is clearly in
S_{y}
. So,
\xi=\sum_{x}\mu(x)\cdotp\sum_{y}\nu_{x}(y)\cdotp\xi_{x,y}
, and therefore
\xi
is in the first set.
∎

Theorem B.8 (Associativity).

(f^{\dagger}\circ g)^{\dagger}=f^{\dagger}\circ g^{\dagger}

Proof.

For any
f\colon Y\to\mathcal{C}(Z)
,
g\colon X\to\mathcal{C}(Y)
, and
S\in\mathcal{C}(X)
:

\displaystyle(f^{\dagger}\circ g)^{\dagger}(S)

\displaystyle=\left\{\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(x)\cdotp\mu^{\prime}_{x}\;\;\Big|\;\;\mu\in S,\forall x.\ \mu^{\prime}_{x}\in(f^{\dagger}\circ g)_{\bot}(x)\right\}

Clearly
(f^{\dagger}\circ g)_{\bot}(x)=f^{\dagger}(g(x))
since
(f^{\dagger}\circ g)_{\bot}(x)=(f^{\dagger}\circ g)(x)=f^{\dagger}(g(x))=f^{\dagger}(g_{\bot}(x))
if
x\in X
, and
(f^{\dagger}\circ g)_{\bot}(\bot)=\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\delta_{\bot}\}}=f^{\dagger}(g_{\bot}(\bot))
. Expanding the second Kleisli extension, we get:

\displaystyle=\left\{\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(x)\cdotp\mu^{\prime}_{x}\;\;\Big|\;\;\mu\in S,\forall x.\ \mu^{\prime}_{x}\in\left\{\sum_{y\in\mathsf{supp}(\nu)}\nu(y)\cdotp\nu^{\prime}_{y}\;\;\Big|\;\;\nu\in g_{\bot}(x),\forall y.\ \nu^{\prime}_{y}\in f_{\bot}(y)\right\}\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(x)\cdot\sum_{y\in\mathsf{supp}(\nu_{x})}\nu_{x}(y)\cdotp\nu^{\prime}_{x,y}\;\;\Big|\;\;\mu\in S,\forall x.\ \nu_{x}\in g_{\bot}(x),\forall y.\ \nu^{\prime}_{x,y}\in f_{\bot}(y)\right\}

By
Lemma

B.7
.

\displaystyle=\left\{{\sum_{y\in\bigcup_{x}\mathsf{supp}(\nu_{x})}}\left(\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(x)\cdotp\nu_{x}\right)\cdotp\nu^{\prime}_{y}\;\;\Big|\;\;\mu\in S,\forall x.\ \nu_{x}\in g_{\bot}(x),\forall y.\ \nu^{\prime}_{y}\in f_{\bot}(y)\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\sum_{y\in\mathsf{supp}(\nu)}\nu(y)\cdotp\nu^{\prime}_{y}\;\;\Big|\;\;\nu\in\left\{\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(x)\cdotp\mu^{\prime}_{x}\;\;\Big|\;\;\mu\in S,\forall x.\ \mu^{\prime}_{x}\in g_{\bot}(x)\right\},\forall y.\ \nu^{\prime}_{y}\in f_{\bot}(y)\right\}

\displaystyle=f^{\dagger}\left(\left\{\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(x)\cdotp\mu^{\prime}_{x}\;\;\Big|\;\;\mu\in S,\forall x.\ \mu^{\prime}_{x}\in g_{\bot}(x)\right\}\right)

\displaystyle=f^{\dagger}(g^{\dagger}(S))

∎

B.3.
Fixed Point Existence

The function
f^{\dagger}
is monotone with respect to
\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}
: if
S\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{C}}T
, then
f^{\dagger}(S)\sqsubseteq f^{\dagger}(T)
. This is true because
f^{\dagger}
is monotone with respect to set inclusion: If
T\mathrel{\subseteq}S
, then
f^{\dagger}(T)\mathrel{\subseteq}f^{\dagger}(S)
, as is obvious from the definition.

Any
\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{C}}
-directed set
D
of elements of
\mathcal{C}(X)
has a supremum
\sup D
, namely
\bigcap D
. This set is nonempty and closed, since it is the intersection of a family of closed sets with the finite intersection property in a compact space; convex, since the intersection of convex sets is convex; and up-closed, since the intersection of up-closed sets is up-closed. Thus
\mathcal{C}(X)
forms a DCPO under
\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{C}}
.

In the following we use
\Pi_{x\in X}
to denote a cartesian product indexed by
x\in X
. For any
y\in\Pi_{x\in X}S_{x}
, we let
y_{x}
be the
x^{\text{th}}
projection of
y
, so that
y_{x}\in S_{x}
.

Lemma B.9.

\prod_{x\in X}\bigcap_{f\in D}f(x)=\bigcap_{f\in D}\prod_{x\in X}f(x)

Proof.

\displaystyle y\in\prod_{x\in X}\bigcap_{f\in D}f(x)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\forall x\in X.~y_{x}\in\bigcap_{f\in D}f(x)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\forall x\in X.~\forall f\in D.~y_{x}\in f(x)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\forall f\in D.~\forall x\in X.~y_{x}\in f(x)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\forall f\in D.~y\in\prod_{x\in X}f(x)

∎

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow y\in\bigcap_{f\in D}\prod_{x\in X}f(x)

Lemma B.10.

(-)_{\bot}\colon(X\to\mathcal{C}(Y))\to X_{\bot}\to\mathcal{C}(Y)
is Scott-continuous with respect to
\sqsubseteq^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{C}}
.

Proof.

First, we show that
(-)_{\bot}
is monotone. Suppose that
f\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}g
. Now take any
x\in X_{\bot}
. If
x\in X
, then we have:

f_{\bot}(x)=f(x)\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{C}}g(x)=g_{\bot}(x)

If instead
x=\bot
, then we have:

f_{\bot}(\bot)=\eta(\bot)=g_{\bot}(\bot)

So in both cases
f_{\bot}\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}g_{\bot}
. Now we show that
(-)_{\bot}
preserves suprema. Let
D\subseteq X\to\mathcal{C}(Y_{\bot})
be a directed set, and take any
x\in X_{\bot}
. If
x\in X
, then we have:

\sup_{f\in D}f_{\bot}(x)=\sup_{f\in D}f(x)=(\sup D)(x)=(\sup D)_{\bot}(x)

If, alternatively,
x=\bot
, then we have:

\sup_{f\in D}f_{\bot}(\bot)=\sup_{f\in D}\eta(\bot)=\eta(\bot)=(\sup D)_{\bot}(\bot)

So, in either case
\sup_{f\in D}f_{\bot}(x)=(\sup D)_{\bot}(x)
.
∎

Lemma B.11.

(-)^{\dagger}\colon(X\to\mathcal{C}(Y))\to\mathcal{C}(X)\to\mathcal{C}(Y)
is Scott-continuous with respect to
\sqsubseteq^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{C}}
in its first argument.

Proof.

\displaystyle(\sup D)^{\dagger}(S)

\displaystyle=\left\{\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(x)\cdotp\nu_{x}\;\;\middle|\;\;\mu\in S,\ \nu\in\prod_{x\in X}(\sup D)_{\bot}(x)\right\}

By
Lemma

B.10
.

\displaystyle=\left\{\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(x)\cdotp\nu_{x}\;\;\middle|\;\;\mu\in S,\ \nu\in\prod_{x\in X}\sup_{f\in D}f_{\bot}(x)\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(x)\cdotp\nu_{x}\;\;\middle|\;\;\mu\in S,\ \nu\in\prod_{x\in X}\bigcap_{f\in D}f_{\bot}(x)\right\}

By
Lemma

B.9
.

\displaystyle=\left\{\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(x)\cdotp\nu_{x}\;\;\middle|\;\;\mu\in S,\ \nu\in\bigcap_{f\in D}\prod_{x\in X}f_{\bot}(x)\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(x)\cdotp\nu_{x}\;\;\middle|\;\;\mu\in S,\ \forall f\in D.~\nu\in\prod_{x\in X}f_{\bot}(x)\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(x)\cdotp\nu_{x}\;\;\middle|\;\;\forall f\in D.~(\mu\in S,\ \nu\in\prod_{x\in X}f_{\bot}(x))\right\}

\displaystyle=\bigcap_{f\in D}\left\{\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(x)\cdotp\nu_{x}\;\;\middle|\;\;\mu\in S,\ \nu\in\prod_{x\in X}f_{\bot}(x)\right\}

\displaystyle=\bigcap_{f\in D}f^{\dagger}(S)=\sup_{f\in D}f^{\dagger}(S)

The third-to-last inference is justified by the logical rule
\varphi\wedge\forall x\ \psi\equiv\forall x\ (\varphi\wedge\psi)
provided
x
is not free in
\varphi
.
∎

Lemma B.12 (Scott Continuity).

The function
\Phi_{\langle C,e\rangle}\colon\Sigma\to\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)
is Scott continuous with respect to
\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}
(the pointwise order).

Proof.

Suppose that
D\subseteq\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)
is a directed set, then for any
\sigma\in\Sigma
we have:

\displaystyle\sup_{f\in D}\Phi_{\langle C,e\rangle}(f)(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\sup_{f\in D}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}f^{\dagger}(\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket(\sigma))&\text{if}~\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(\sigma)=\mathsf{true}\\
\eta(\sigma)&\text{if}~\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(\sigma)=\mathsf{false}\end{array}\right.

Since
f
is not free in
e
or
\sigma
.

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\sup_{f\in D}f^{\dagger}(\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket(\sigma))&\text{if}~\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(\sigma)=\mathsf{true}\\
\eta(\sigma)&\text{if}~\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(\sigma)=\mathsf{false}\end{array}\right.

By
Lemma

B.11
.

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(\sup D)^{\dagger}(\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket(\sigma))&\text{if}~\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(\sigma)=\mathsf{true}\\
\eta(\sigma)&\text{if}~\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(\sigma)=\mathsf{false}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle=\Phi_{\langle C,e\rangle}(\sup D)(\sigma)

∎

B.4.
Countability of State Space

Lemma B.13.

For any program
C
and initial state
\sigma\in\Sigma
,
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket(\sigma)
can only reach countably many states. That is,
\tau\in\Sigma_{\bot}
for any distribution
\mu\in\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket(\sigma)
and any
\tau\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)
, and
\Sigma_{\bot}
is a countable set.

Proof.

We first recall that:

\Sigma_{\bot}=\Sigma\cup\{\bot\}=(\mathsf{Var}\to\mathsf{Val})\cup\{\bot\}

Recall from
Section

3.1
that
\mathsf{Val}
contains Booleans and rationals, so it is a countable set. We later add finite length lists in
Section

E.1
, which preserves countability. The set
\mathsf{Var}
is finite, making
\Sigma_{\bot}
as a whole countable.

The proof is by induction of the structure of
C
. In the base case where
C=\texttt{skip}
, the claim follows trivially, since the only reachable state is
\sigma
. If
C=x\coloneqq e
, then the program reaches the state
\sigma[x\coloneqq\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(\sigma)]
. We presume that
e
can only perform Boolean logic, rational arithmetic, and operations on finite length lists (see
Section

E.1
), meaning that
\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\in\mathsf{Val}
, and so
\sigma[x\coloneqq\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(\sigma)]\in\Sigma
.

For sequential composition, we have:

\left\llbracket C_{1}\fatsemi C_{2}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)=\left\{\sum_{\tau\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(\tau)\cdot\nu_{\tau}\;\;\Big|\;\;\mu\in\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket(\sigma),\forall\tau\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu).~\nu_{\tau}\in\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket(\tau)\right\}

Bu the induction hypothesis, we know that
\mathsf{supp}(\mu)\subseteq\Sigma_{\bot}
for each
\mu\in\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)
. By the induction hypothesis again, we get that
\mathsf{supp}(\nu_{\tau})\subseteq\Sigma_{\bot}
for each
\tau\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)
. Since any distribution in
\left\llbracket C_{1}\fatsemi C_{2}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)
is obtained from combinations of those
\nu_{\tau}
, all the states are again contained in
\Sigma_{\bot}
.

In the other two composition operators
\left\llbracket C_{1}\oplus_{p}C_{2}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)
and
\left\llbracket C_{1}\mathbin{\&}C_{2}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)
and if statements, we use the induction hypothesis to conclude that the claim holds for
\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)
and
\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)
. The final distributions are then convex combinations of those smaller distributions, so the claim again holds.

Finally, for loops we have:

\left\llbracket\texttt{while}~e~\texttt{do}~C\right\rrbracket(\sigma)=\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\Phi^{n}_{\langle C,e\rangle}(\bot_{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet})(\sigma)=\bot_{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}(\sigma)\cap\Phi_{\langle C,e\rangle}(\bot_{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet})(\sigma)\cap\cdots\subseteq\mathcal{D}(\Sigma_{\bot})

More precisely, since the semantics involves taking an intersection with
\bot_{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}(\sigma)=\mathcal{D}(\Sigma_{\bot})
, we know that the resulting set cannot contain terms outside of
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma_{\bot})
, meaning that the support of every distribution in this sequence must by contained in
\Sigma_{\bot}
.
∎

Appendix C
Logical Rules

C.1.
Nondeterministic and Probabilistic Choice

Lemma C.1.

For any function
f\colon\Sigma\to\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)
and set
S\in\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)
:

f^{\dagger}(S)=\bigcup_{\mu\in S}f^{\dagger}(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}})

Proof.

\displaystyle f^{\dagger}(S)

\displaystyle=\left\{\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(\sigma)\cdot\nu_{\sigma}\;\;\Big|\;\;\mu\in S,\forall\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu).~\nu_{\sigma}\in f_{\bot}(\sigma)\right\}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{\mu\in S}\left\{\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(\sigma)\cdot\nu_{\sigma}\;\;\Big|\;\;\forall\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu).~\nu_{\sigma}\in f_{\bot}(\sigma)\right\}

Now, since
S
is up-closed, then
\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}}\subseteq S
for each
\mu\in S
. Since set union is idempotent, we can expand the union as follows:

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{\mu\in S}\bigcup_{\mu^{\prime}\in\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}}}\left\{\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu^{\prime})}\mu^{\prime}(\sigma)\cdot\nu_{\sigma}\;\;\Big|\;\;\forall\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu^{\prime}).~\nu_{\sigma}\in f_{\bot}(\sigma)\right\}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{\mu\in S}\left\{\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(\sigma)\cdot\nu_{\sigma}\;\;\Big|\;\;\mu^{\prime}\in\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}},\forall\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu^{\prime}).~\nu_{\sigma}\in f_{\bot}(\sigma)\right\}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{\mu\in S}f^{\dagger}(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}})

∎

Lemma C.2.

For any function
f\colon X\to\mathcal{C}(Y)
, probability
p\in[0,1]
, and sets
S,T\in\mathcal{C}(X)
.

f^{\dagger}(S\oplus_{p}T)=f^{\dagger}(S)\oplus_{p}f^{\dagger}(T)

Proof.

Let
\xi\in\mathcal{D}(\{1,2\})
be a distribution where
\xi(1)=p
and
\xi(2)=1-p
. Let
g\colon\{1,2\}\to\mathcal{C}(X)
be defined as
g(1)=S
and
g(2)=T
. Now, we have:

\displaystyle f^{\dagger}(S\oplus_{p}T)

\displaystyle=f^{\dagger}(\{p\cdot\mu+(1-p)\cdot\nu\mid\mu\in S,\nu\in T\})

\displaystyle=f^{\dagger}(\{\xi(1)\cdot\mu_{1}+\xi(2)\cdot\mu_{2}\mid\forall i\in\{1,2\}.\ \mu_{i}\in g(i)\})

\displaystyle=f^{\dagger}(g^{\dagger}(\{\xi\}))

By the monad laws:

\displaystyle=(f^{\dagger}\circ g)^{\dagger}(\{\xi\})

\displaystyle=\{\xi(1)\cdot\mu_{1}+\xi(2)\cdot\mu_{2}\mid\forall i\in\{1,2\}.\ \mu_{i}\in f^{\dagger}(g(i))\}

\displaystyle=\{p\cdot\mu_{1}+(1-p)\cdot\mu_{2}\mid\mu_{1}\in f^{\dagger}(S),\mu_{2}\in f^{\dagger}(T)\}=f^{\dagger}(S)\oplus_{p}f^{\dagger}(T)

∎

Lemma C.3.

Let
\mu=p\cdot\mu_{1}+(1-p)\cdot\mu_{2}
.
If
\mu\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{D}}\nu
, then there exists
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
such that
\nu=p\cdot\nu_{1}+(1-p)\cdot\nu_{2}
and
\mu_{i}\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{D}}\nu_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
.

Proof.

If
\mu(\bot)=0
, the claim is trivial since
\mu=\nu
and thus we can simply let
\nu_{i}=\mu_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
. If instead
\mu(\bot)>0
, then for every
\sigma\in\Sigma
and
i\in\{1,2\}
, let:

\nu_{i}(\sigma)\triangleq\mu_{i}(\sigma)+\frac{\mu_{i}(\bot)}{\mu(\bot)}\cdot(\nu(\sigma)-\mu(\sigma))

First, we must establish that
\nu_{i}
is a probability distribution. Note that
\nu_{i}(\sigma)
is nonnegative for all
\sigma\in\Sigma
, since
\nu(\sigma)\geq\mu(\sigma)
, and thus the two terms being summed are both nonnegative. In addition, we have:

\displaystyle\nu_{i}(\bot)=\mu_{i}(\bot)+\frac{\mu_{i}(\bot)}{\mu(\bot)}\cdot(\nu(\bot)-\mu(\bot))=\mu_{i}(\bot)\cdot\left(\cancel{1}+\frac{\nu(\bot)}{\mu(\bot)}-\cancel{1}\right)=\mu_{i}(\bot)\cdot\frac{\nu(\bot)}{\mu(\bot)}

Which is clearly nonnegative too. Now, we also have:

\displaystyle|\nu_{i}|

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\nu_{i})}\nu_{i}(\sigma)

We can expand the bounds of the sum, since
\nu_{i}(\tau)=0
for any
\tau\notin\mathsf{supp}(\nu_{i})
, and therefore including the extra terms will not affect the value.

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{\bot}}\nu_{i}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{\bot}}\mu_{i}(\sigma)+\frac{\mu_{i}(\bot)}{\mu(\bot)}\cdot(\nu(\sigma)-\mu(\sigma))

\displaystyle=\left({\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{\bot}}}\mu_{i}(\sigma)\right)+\frac{\mu_{i}(\bot)}{\mu(\bot)}\cdot\left(\left({\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{\bot}}}\nu(\sigma)\right)-\left({\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{\bot}}}\mu(\sigma)\right)\right)

\displaystyle=1+\frac{\mu_{i}(\bot)}{\mu(\bot)}\cdot(1-1)=1+\frac{\mu_{i}(\bot)}{\mu(\bot)}\cdot 0=1

So, since all the points are nonnegative and the total mass of
\nu_{i}
is 1, then all the points are in-bounds and
\nu_{i}
is a valid distribution. In addition, since
\nu(\sigma)\geq\mu(\sigma)
for all
\sigma\in\Sigma
, then
\nu(\sigma)-\mu(\sigma)\geq 0
and so:

\nu_{i}(\sigma)=\mu_{i}(\sigma)+\frac{\mu_{i}(\bot)}{\mu(\bot)}\cdot(\nu(\sigma)-\mu(\sigma))\geq\mu_{i}(\sigma)+\frac{\mu_{i}(\bot)}{\mu(\bot)}\cdot 0=\mu_{i}(\sigma)

So we have that
\mu_{i}\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{D}}\nu_{i}
. And finally, for any
\sigma\in\Sigma_{\bot}
:

\displaystyle p\cdot\nu_{1}(\sigma)+(1-p)\cdot\nu_{2}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=p\cdot\left(\mu_{1}(\sigma)+\frac{\mu_{1}(\bot)}{\mu(\bot)}\cdot(\nu(\sigma)-\mu(\sigma))\right)+(1-p)\cdot\left(\mu_{2}(\sigma)+\frac{\mu_{2}(\bot)}{\mu(\bot)}\cdot(\nu(\sigma)-\mu(\sigma))\right)

\displaystyle=\left(p\cdot\mu_{1}(\sigma)+(1-p)\cdot\mu_{2}(\sigma)\right)+(p\cdot\mu_{1}(\bot)+(1-p)\cdot\mu_{2}(\bot))\cdot\frac{\nu(\sigma)-\mu(\sigma)}{\mu(\bot)}

\displaystyle=\mu(\sigma)+\cancel{\mu(\bot)}\cdot\frac{\nu(\sigma)-\mu(\sigma)}{\cancel{\mu(\bot)}}=\mu(\sigma)+\nu(\sigma)-\mu(\sigma)=\nu(\sigma)

∎

Lemma C.4.

\mathord{\uparrow}{\{p\cdot\mu_{1}+(1-p)\cdot\mu_{2}\}}=\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu_{1}\}}\oplus_{p}\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu_{2}\}}

Proof.

First, we will show that
\mathord{\uparrow}{\{p\cdot\mu_{1}+(1-p)\cdot\mu_{2}\}}\subseteq\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu_{1}\}}\oplus_{p}\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu_{2}\}}
. Take some
\nu\in\mathord{\uparrow}{\{p\cdot\mu_{1}+(1-p)\cdot\mu_{2}\}}
. By
Lemma

C.3
we know there must be
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
such that
\mu_{i}\sqsubseteq\nu_{i}
and
\nu=p\cdot\nu_{1}+(1-p)\cdot\nu_{2}
. So,
\nu_{i}\in\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu_{i}\}}
, and therefore also
\nu\in\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu_{1}\}}\oplus_{p}\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu_{2}\}}
.

Now, we show that
\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu_{1}\}}\oplus_{p}\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu_{2}\}}\subseteq\mathord{\uparrow}{\{p\cdot\mu_{1}+(1-p)\cdot\mu_{2}\}}
. Take some
\nu\in\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu_{1}\}}\oplus_{p}\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu_{2}\}}
. So, there is a
\nu_{1}\in\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu_{1}\}}
and
\nu_{2}\in\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu_{2}\}}
such that
\nu=p\cdot\nu_{1}+(1-p)\cdot\nu_{2}
. Take any
\sigma\in\Sigma
, we have that:

\displaystyle\nu(\sigma)

\displaystyle=p\cdot\nu_{1}(\sigma)+(1-p)\cdot\nu_{2}(\sigma)

\displaystyle\geq p\cdot\mu_{1}(\sigma)+(1-p)\cdot\mu_{2}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=(p\cdot\mu_{1}+(1-p)\cdot\mu_{2})(\sigma)

Therefore
p\cdot\mu_{1}+(1-p)\cdot\mu_{2}\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{D}}\nu
and so
\nu\in\mathord{\uparrow}{\{p\cdot\mu_{1}+(1-p)\cdot\mu_{2}\}}
.
∎

Lemma C.5.

For any sequence of distributions
(\mu_{i})_{i\in I}
, any
\xi\in\mathcal{D}(I)
, and any assertion
\varphi

, if
\mu_{i}\vDash\varphi
for each
i\in I
, then
\sum_{i\in I}\xi(i)\cdot\mu_{i}\vDash\varphi
.

Proof.

By induction on the structure of
\varphi

\triangleright

\varphi=\top
. Trivial since
\sum_{i\in I}\xi(i)\cdot\mu_{i}\vDash\top
always.

\triangleright

\varphi=\bot
. Vacuous since
\mu_{i}\vDash\bot
is impossible.

\triangleright

\varphi=\varphi_{1}\land\varphi_{2}
. We know that
\mu_{i}\vDash\varphi_{1}
and
\mu_{i}\vDash\varphi_{2}
for each
i\in I
. So, by the induction hypothesis,
\sum_{i\in I}\xi(i)\cdot\mu_{i}\vDash\varphi_{1}
and
\sum_{i\in I}\xi(i)\cdot\mu_{i}\vDash\varphi_{2}
, therefore
\sum_{i\in I}\xi(i)\cdot\mu_{i}\vDash\varphi_{1}\land\varphi_{2}
.

\triangleright

\varphi=\varphi_{1}\mathbin{\&}\varphi_{2}
. So for each
i\in I
, there exists
(\nu_{i,j})_{j\in\{1,2\}}
and
p_{i}\in[0,1]
such that
\mu_{i}=p_{i}\cdot\nu_{i,1}+(1-p_{i})\cdot\nu_{i,2}
and
\nu_{i,j}\vDash\varphi_{j}
for each
j\in\{1,2\}
.
Now, let
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\mathcal{D}(I)
be defined as follows:

\xi_{1}(i)\triangleq\frac{p_{i}\cdot\xi(i)}{\sum_{k\in I}p_{k}\cdot\xi(k)}\qquad\xi_{2}(i)\triangleq\frac{(1-p_{i})\cdot\xi(i)}{\sum_{k\in I}(1-p_{k})\cdot\xi(k)}

And by the induction hypothesis, we know that
\sum_{i\in I}\xi_{j}(i)\cdot\nu_{i,j}\vDash\varphi_{j}
.
Now, observe that:

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I}\xi(i)\cdot\mu_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in I}\xi(i)\cdot(p_{i}\cdot\nu_{i,1}+(1-p_{i})\cdot\nu_{i,2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in I}p_{i}\cdot\xi(i)\cdot\nu_{i,1}+\sum_{i\in I}(1-p_{i})\cdot\xi(i)\cdot\nu_{i,2}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{k\in I}p_{k}\cdot\xi(k)\right)\cdot\sum_{i\in I}\xi_{1}(i)\cdot\nu_{i,1}+\left(\sum_{k\in I}(1-p_{k})\cdot\xi(k)\right)\cdot\sum_{i\in I}\xi_{2}(i)\cdot\nu_{i,2}

Therefore
\sum_{i\in I}\xi(i)\cdot\mu_{i}\vDash\varphi_{1}\mathbin{\&}\varphi_{2}
.

\triangleright

\varphi=\varphi_{1}\oplus_{p}\varphi_{2}
. We know that
\mu_{i}=p\cdot\mu_{i,1}+(1-p)\cdot\mu_{i,2}
such that
\mu_{i,j}\vDash\varphi_{j}
for
j\in\{1,2\}
and
i\in I
. By the induction hypothesis, we therefore get that
\sum_{i\in I}\xi(i)\cdot\mu_{i,j}\vDash\varphi_{j}
for each
j\in\{1,2\}
. Now, we have:

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I}\xi(i)\cdot\mu_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in I}\xi(i)\cdot(p\cdot\mu_{i,1}+(1-p)\cdot\mu_{i,2}

\displaystyle=p\cdot\sum_{i\in I}\xi(i)\cdot\mu_{i,1}+(1-p)\cdot\sum_{i\in I}\xi(i)\cdot\mu_{i,2})

Therefore
\sum_{i\in I}\xi(i)\cdot\mu_{i}\vDash\varphi_{1}\oplus_{p}\varphi_{2}
.

\triangleright

\varphi=P
. We know that
\mathsf{supp}(\mu_{i})\subseteq\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis
for each
i\in I
. Therefore, we have:

\mathsf{supp}(\sum_{i\in I}\xi(i)\cdot\mu_{i})=\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathsf{supp}(\mu_{i})\subseteq\bigcup_{i\in I}\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis=\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis

Therefore
\sum_{i\in I}\xi(i)\cdot\mu_{i}\vDash P
.∎

C.2.
Soundness of the Sequential Proof Rules

Lemma C.6 (Monotonicity).

If
\mu\vDash\varphi
, then
\nu\vDash\varphi
for any
\nu\in\mathcal{D}(\Sigma_{\bot})
such that
\mu\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{D}}\nu
.

Proof.

By induction on the structure of
\varphi
.

\triangleright

\varphi=\top
. Trivial since
\nu\vDash\top
always.

\triangleright

\varphi=\bot
. Vacuous, since
\mu\vDash\bot
is impossible.

\triangleright

\varphi=\varphi_{1}\land\varphi_{2}
. We know that
\mu\vDash\varphi_{1}
and
\mu\vDash\varphi_{2}
. Now take any
\nu
such that
\mu\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{D}}\nu
. By the induction hypothesis, we know that
\nu\vDash\varphi_{1}
and
\nu\vDash\varphi_{2}
, therefore
\nu\vDash\varphi_{1}\land\varphi_{2}
.

\triangleright

\varphi=\varphi_{1}\oplus_{p}\varphi_{2}
. We know that
\mu=p\cdot\varphi_{1}+(1-p)\cdot\varphi_{2}
such that
\mu_{i}\vDash\varphi_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
. Now take any
\nu
such that
\mu\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{D}}\nu
. By
Lemma

C.3
we know that there is
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
such that
\nu=p\cdot\nu_{1}+(1-p)\cdot\nu_{2}
and
\nu_{i}(\sigma)\geq\mu_{i}(\sigma)
