too. So, we can use the induction hypothesis to conclude that
\nu_{i}\vDash\varphi_{i}
, and so
\nu\vDash\varphi_{1}\oplus_{p}\varphi_{2}
.

\triangleright

\varphi=\varphi_{1}\mathbin{\&}\varphi_{2}
. We know that
\mu\vDash\varphi_{1}\oplus_{p}\varphi_{2}
for some
p\in[0,1]
. Now take any
\nu
such that
\mu\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{D}}\nu
. By the previous case, we know that
\nu\vDash\varphi_{1}\oplus_{p}\varphi_{2}
. Since
\varphi_{1}\oplus_{p}\varphi_{2}\Rightarrow\varphi_{1}\mathbin{\&}\varphi_{2}
, then
\nu\vDash\varphi_{1}\mathbin{\&}\varphi_{2}
.

\triangleright

\varphi=\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
. Since
\mathsf{supp}(\mu)\subseteq\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis\subseteq\Sigma
, we know that
\bot\notin\mathsf{supp}(\mu)
. Therefore, if
\mu\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{D}}\nu
, then
\mu=\nu
, since there is not distribution strictly larger than
\mu
, and so
\nu\vDash\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
.∎

Lemma C.7.

If
\mu\vDash\varphi[e/x]
then
\nu\vDash\varphi
for all
\nu\in\left\llbracket x\coloneqq e\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}})
.

Proof.

By induction on the structure of
\varphi
.

\triangleright

\varphi=\top
. it is trivial that
\nu\vDash\top
for any
\nu
.

\triangleright

\varphi=\bot
. This case is vacuous, since
\bot[e/x]=\bot
and therefore the premise that
\mu\vDash\bot[e/x]
is impossible.

\triangleright

\varphi=\varphi_{1}\land\varphi_{2}
. We know that
(\varphi_{1}\land\varphi_{2})[e/x]=\varphi_{1}[e/x]\land\varphi_{2}[e/x]
, and so
\mu\vDash\varphi_{i}[e/x]
for
i\in\{1,2\}
. Now, take any
\nu\in\left\llbracket x\coloneqq e\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}})
. By the induction hypothesis, we get that
\nu\vDash\varphi_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
, therefore
\nu\vDash\varphi_{1}\land\varphi_{2}
.

\triangleright

\varphi=\varphi_{1}\oplus_{p}\varphi_{2}
. We know that
\mu=p\cdot\mu_{1}+(1-p)\cdot\mu_{2}
such that
\mu_{i}\vDash\varphi_{i}[e/x]
for
i\in\{1,2\}
. Now, by
Lemmas

C.4
and
C.2
. we get that:

\left\llbracket x\coloneqq e\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}})=\left\llbracket x\coloneqq e\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu_{1}\}}\oplus_{p}\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu_{2}\}})=\left\llbracket x\coloneqq e\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu_{1}\}})\oplus_{p}\left\llbracket x\coloneqq e\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu_{2}\}})

And therefore any
\nu\in\left\llbracket x\coloneqq e\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}})
must have the form
p\cdot\nu_{1}+(1-p)\cdot\nu_{2}
where
\nu_{i}\in\left\llbracket x\coloneqq e\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}_{\bot}(\{\mu_{i}\})
for
i\in\{1,2\}
. So, by the induction hypothesis,
\nu_{i}\vDash\varphi_{i}
, and therefore
\nu\vDash\varphi_{1}\oplus_{p}\varphi_{2}
.

\triangleright

\varphi=\varphi_{1}\mathbin{\&}\varphi_{2}
. We know that there is some
p
such that
\mu\vDash(\varphi_{1}\oplus_{p}\varphi_{2})[e/x]
, so by the previous case, we get that for any
\nu\vDash\varphi_{1}\oplus_{p}\varphi_{2}
for any
\nu\in\left\llbracket x\coloneqq e\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}})
. Since
\varphi_{1}\oplus_{p}\varphi_{2}\Rightarrow\varphi_{1}\mathbin{\&}\varphi_{2}
, then
\nu\vDash\varphi_{1}\mathbin{\&}\varphi_{2}
as well.

\triangleright

\varphi=\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
. We know that
\mathsf{supp}(\mu)\subseteq\llparenthesis P[e/x]\rrparenthesis
. Now, take any
\nu\in\left\llbracket x\coloneqq e\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}})
. Any
\tau\in\mathsf{supp}(\nu)
must have the form
\sigma[x\coloneqq\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(\sigma)]
for some
\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)
. Since
\sigma\in\llparenthesis P[e/x]\rrparenthesis
, it must be that
\sigma[x\coloneqq\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(\sigma)]\in\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis
.∎

See
4.2

Proof.

By induction on the derivation.

\triangleright

Skip
. Suppose
\mu\vDash\varphi
. Note that
\left\llbracket\texttt{skip}\right\rrbracket=\eta
and so
\left\llbracket\texttt{skip}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}=\mathsf{id}
, therefore
\left\llbracket\texttt{skip}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}})=\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}}
.
Now, take any
\nu\in\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}}
, clearly
\mu\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{D}}\nu
, so
\nu\vDash\varphi
by
Lemma

C.6
.

\triangleright

Assign
. Follows from
Lemma

C.7
.

\triangleright

Seq
. By the induction hypothesis, we know that
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\vartheta{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
and
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\vartheta{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
and we have to show that
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{1}\fatsemi C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
. Suppose that
\mu\vDash\varphi
, and observe that:

\displaystyle\left\llbracket C_{1}\fatsemi C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}})

\displaystyle=(\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}_{\bot}\circ\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\bot})^{\dagger}(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}})

\displaystyle=\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}}))

(By
Lemma

C.1
.)

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{\nu\in\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}})}\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\nu\}})

So, for any
\xi\in\left\llbracket C_{1}\fatsemi C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}})
, there must be a
\nu\in\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}})
such that
\xi\in\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\nu\}})
. From
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\vartheta{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
, we know that
\nu\vDash\vartheta
and therefore from
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\vartheta{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
we get that
\xi\vDash\psi
.

\triangleright

Prob
. Suppose
\mu\vDash\varphi
, therefore since
\varphi\Rightarrow(e=p)
, we know that
\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(\sigma)=p
for every
\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)
.
Note that this also means that
\bot\notin\mathsf{supp}(\mu)
and so
\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}}=\{\mu\}
.
Now, we have:

\displaystyle\left\llbracket C_{1}\oplus_{p}C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}})

\displaystyle=\left\llbracket C_{1}\oplus_{p}C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\{\mu\})

\displaystyle=\left\{\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(\sigma)\cdot\nu_{\sigma}\;\;\middle|\;\;\forall\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu).~\nu_{\sigma}\in\left\llbracket C_{1}\oplus_{e}C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\bot}(\sigma)\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(\sigma)\cdot(\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot\nu_{\sigma}+(1-\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(\sigma))\cdot\nu^{\prime}_{\sigma})\;\;\middle|\;\;\forall\sigma.~\nu_{\sigma}\in\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\bot}(\sigma),\nu^{\prime}_{\sigma}\in\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\bot}(\sigma)\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{p\cdot\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(\sigma)\cdot\nu_{\sigma}+(1-p)\cdot\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(\sigma)\cdot\nu^{\prime}_{\sigma}\;\;\middle|\;\;\forall\sigma.~\nu_{\sigma}\in\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\bot}(\sigma),\nu^{\prime}_{\sigma}\in\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\bot}(\sigma)\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{p\cdot\nu+(1-p)\cdot\nu^{\prime}\;\;\middle|\;\;\nu\in\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}}),\nu^{\prime}\in\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}})\right\}

So for each
\xi\in\left\llbracket C_{1}\oplus_{p}C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}})
there is a
\nu\in\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}})
and
\nu^{\prime}\in\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}})
such that
\xi=p\cdot\nu+(1-p)\cdot\nu^{\prime}
. We know that
\mu\vDash\varphi
,so by the induction hypothesis
\nu\vDash\psi_{1}
and
\nu^{\prime}\vDash\psi_{2}
, therefore
\xi\vDash\psi_{1}\oplus_{p}\psi_{2}
.

\triangleright

Nondet
. Suppose
\mu\vDash\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
, therefore
\mathsf{supp}(\mu)\subseteq\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis
, and so
\{\delta_{\sigma}\}\vDash\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
for each
\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)
. By the induction hypotheses, we also get that
\left\llbracket C_{i}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\vDash\psi_{i}
for each
\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)
and
i\in\{1,2\}
. This also means that
\nu\vDash\psi_{1}\mathbin{\&}\psi_{2}
for each
\nu\in\left\llbracket C_{1}\mathbin{\&}C_{2}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)
.
Now, we have:

\displaystyle\left\llbracket C_{1}\mathbin{\&}C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}})

\displaystyle=\left\{\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu^{\prime})}\mu^{\prime}(\sigma)\cdot\nu_{\sigma}\;\;\middle|\;\;\mu^{\prime}\in\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}},~\forall\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu^{\prime}).~\nu_{\sigma}\in\left\llbracket C_{1}\mathbin{\&}C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\bot}(\sigma)\right\}

So, taking any
\xi\in\left\llbracket C_{1}\mathbin{\&}C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}})
, we know that
\xi={\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu^{\prime})}}\mu^{\prime}(\sigma)\cdot\nu_{\sigma}
for some
\mu^{\prime}\in\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}}
(note that
\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}}=\{\mu\}
since
\mu(\bot)=0
) where each
\nu_{\sigma}\in\left\llbracket C_{1}\mathbin{\&}C_{2}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)
. That means that
\nu_{\sigma}\vDash\psi_{1}\mathbin{\&}\psi_{2}
and so by
Lemma

C.5
,
\left\llbracket C_{1}\mathbin{\&}C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}})\vDash\psi_{1}\mathbin{\&}\psi_{2}
.

\triangleright

If1
Suppose that
\mu\vDash\varphi
. From
\varphi\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
, we know that
\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(\sigma)=\mathsf{true}
for each
\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)
. Also note that
\mu(\bot)=0
, so
\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}}=\{\mu\}
. Therefore, we get:

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\texttt{if}~e~\texttt{then}~C_{1}~\texttt{else}~C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\{\mu\})

\displaystyle=\{\textstyle\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(\sigma)\cdot\nu_{\sigma}\mid\forall\sigma.~\nu_{\sigma}\in\left\llbracket\texttt{if}~e~\texttt{then}~C_{1}~\texttt{else}~C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\bot}(\sigma)\}

Since
\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(\sigma)=\mathsf{true}
for all
\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)
, then
\left\llbracket\texttt{if}~e~\texttt{then}~C_{1}~\texttt{else}~C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\bot}(\sigma)=\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\bot}(\sigma)
.

\displaystyle=\{\textstyle\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(\sigma)\cdot\nu_{\sigma}\mid\forall\sigma.~\nu_{\sigma}\in\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\bot}(\sigma)\}

\displaystyle=\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\{\mu\})

Now take any
\nu\in\left\llbracket\texttt{if}~e~\texttt{then}~C_{1}~\texttt{else}~C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\{\mu\})
, we know that
\nu\in\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\{\mu\})
, therefore by the induction hypothesis
\nu\vDash\psi
.

\triangleright

If2
. Symmetric to the
If1
case.

\triangleright

Prob Choice
. Suppose
\mu\vDash\varphi_{1}\oplus_{p}\varphi_{2}
, so
\mu=p\cdot\mu_{1}+(1-p)\cdot\mu_{2}
such that
\mu_{i}\vDash\varphi_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
. Now, we have:

\displaystyle\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\{\mu\})

\displaystyle=\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{p\cdot\mu_{1}+(1-p)\cdot\mu_{2}\}})

(By
Lemma

C.4
.)

\displaystyle=\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu_{1}\}}\oplus_{p}\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu_{2}\}})

(By
Lemma

C.2
.)

\displaystyle=\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu_{1}\}})\oplus_{p}\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu_{2}\}})

So, for any
\nu\in\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}})
, it must be that
\nu=p\cdot\nu_{1}+(1-p)\cdot\nu_{2}
such that
\nu_{i}\in\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu_{i}\}})
for
i\in\{1,2\}
. By the induction hypothesis, we know that each
\nu_{i}\vDash\psi_{i}
, therefore
\nu\vDash\psi_{1}\oplus_{p}\psi_{2}
.

\triangleright

ND Choice
.
Suppose
\mu\vDash\varphi_{1}\mathbin{\&}\varphi_{2}
, so we know there is some
p\in[0,1]
such that
\mu\vDash\varphi_{1}\oplus_{p}\varphi_{2}
. Now, take any
\nu\in\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}})
. We know from the
Prob Choice
case that
\nu\vDash\psi_{1}\oplus_{p}\psi_{2}
. Since
\psi_{1}\oplus_{p}\psi_{2}\Rightarrow\psi_{1}\mathbin{\&}\psi_{2}
, then
\nu\vDash\psi_{1}\mathbin{\&}\psi_{2}
as well.

\triangleright

Consequence
. Suppose
\mu\vDash\varphi^{\prime}
, then since
\varphi^{\prime}\Rightarrow\varphi
,
\mu\vDash\varphi
. By the induction hypothesis,
\nu\vDash\psi
for any
\nu\in\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}})
. Finally, since
\psi\Rightarrow\psi^{\prime}
,
\nu\vDash\psi^{\prime}
.

\triangleright

Constancy
. Suppose that
\mu\vDash\varphi\land\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
, so
\mu\vDash\varphi
and
\mathsf{supp}(\mu)\subseteq\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis
. Now, take any
\nu\in\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}})
. By the induction hypothesis, we know that
\nu\vDash\psi
. In addition, since
\mathsf{mod}(C)\cap\mathsf{fv}(P)=\varnothing
, then the program cannot have changed the truth of
P
, and so
\mathsf{supp}(\nu)\subseteq\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis
as well. This means that
\nu\vDash\psi\land\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
.

∎

C.3.
Derived Rules

In this section we give derivations for the additional rules shown in
Section

4.3
.

Lemma C.8.

The following inference is derivable:

\frac{\varphi_{1}\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\quad\varphi_{2}\Rightarrow\lnot\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{1}\oplus_{p}\varphi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{if}~e~\texttt{then}~C_{1}~\texttt{else}~C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi_{1}\oplus_{p}\psi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}

Proof.

\frac{\frac{\varphi_{1}\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\hskip 8.19447pt{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{if}~e~\texttt{then}~C_{1}~\texttt{else}~C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\quad\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:If1}{{If1}}}\quad\frac{\varphi_{2}\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lnot e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\hskip 8.19447pt{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{if}~e~\texttt{then}~C_{1}~\texttt{else}~C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\quad\textsc{Right=\hyperlink{rule:If2}{{If2}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{1}\oplus_{p}\varphi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{if}~e~\texttt{then}~C_{1}~\texttt{else}~C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi_{1}\oplus_{p}\psi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\quad\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Prob Split}{{Prob Split}}}

∎

Lemma C.9.

The following inference is derivable:

\frac{\varphi_{1}\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\quad\varphi_{2}\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lnot e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{1}\mathbin{\&}\varphi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{if}~e~\texttt{then}~C_{1}~\texttt{else}~C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi_{1}\mathbin{\&}\psi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}

Proof.

\frac{\frac{\varphi_{1}\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\hskip 8.19447pt{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{if}~e~\texttt{then}~C_{1}~\texttt{else}~C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\quad\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:If1}{{If1}}}\quad\frac{\varphi_{2}\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lnot e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\hskip 8.19447pt{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{if}~e~\texttt{then}~C_{1}~\texttt{else}~C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\quad\textsc{Right=\hyperlink{rule:If2}{{If2}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{1}\mathbin{\&}\varphi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{if}~e~\texttt{then}~C_{1}~\texttt{else}~C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi_{1}\mathbin{\&}\psi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\quad\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:ND Split}{{ND Split}}}

∎

Lemma C.10.

The following inference is derivable:

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\land\lnot e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{if}~e~\texttt{then}~C_{1}~\texttt{else}~C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}

Proof.

First, note that
\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\land e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\mathbin{\&}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\land\lnot e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
, since for any
\mu\vDash\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
,
e
must be either true or false in every
\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)
. We can therefore complete the derivation as follows:

\frac{\frac{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\hskip 8.19447pt{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\land e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\hskip 8.19447pt\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\land\lnot e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lnot e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\hskip 8.19447pt{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\land\lnot e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\mathbin{\&}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\land\lnot e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{if}~e~\texttt{then}~C_{1}~\texttt{else}~C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi\mathbin{\&}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\quad\textsc{Right=\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{lem:if-nd}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{if}~e~\texttt{then}~C_{1}~\texttt{else}~C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\quad\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Consequence}{{Consequence}}}

∎

Lemma C.11.

The following inference is derivable:

\frac{\varphi\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e=p\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\quad x\notin\mathsf{fv}(\varphi)}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x\coloneqq\mathsf{flip}(e)~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi\land(\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{p}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x=\mathsf{false}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}

Proof.

First, note that since
x\notin\mathsf{fv}(\varphi)
, then
\varphi[v/x]=\varphi
for any
v\in\mathsf{Val}
. Therefore, we have:

\left(\varphi\land\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\right)[\mathsf{true}/x]\quad=\quad\varphi[\mathsf{true}/x]\land\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{true}=\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\quad=\quad\varphi

And similarly for the
x=\mathsf{false}
case. We now complete the derivation as follows:

\frac{\frac{\frac{\;}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x\coloneqq\mathsf{true}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi\land\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Assign}{{Assign}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\frac{\;}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x\coloneqq\mathsf{false}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi\land\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=\mathsf{false}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{Right=\hyperlink{rule:Assign}{{Assign}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~(x\coloneqq\mathsf{true})\oplus_{e}(x\coloneqq\mathsf{false})~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}(\varphi\land\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil)\oplus_{p}(\varphi\land\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x=\mathsf{false}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\quad\textsc{Right=\hyperlink{rule:Prob}{{Prob}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x\coloneqq\mathsf{flip}(e)~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi\land(\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{p}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x=\mathsf{false}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\quad\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Consequence}{{Consequence}}}

∎

Lemma C.12.

The following inference is derivable:

\frac{\;}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x\leftarrow S~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\textstyle\bignd_{v\in S}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x=v\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}

Proof.

Recall that
S
must be a nonempty finite set, so let
S=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\}
, and therefore the postcondition is equivalent to
\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=v_{1}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\mathbin{\&}\cdots\mathbin{\&}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x=v_{n}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
. We now proceed by induction on the size of the set
S
. In the base case,
|S|=1
and so
S=\{v\}
is a singleton set. We therefore the complete the derivation with a single application of the
Assign
rule since
\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=v\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil[v/x]=\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}v=v\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil=\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
.

\frac{\;}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x\coloneqq v~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x=v\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\quad\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Assign}{{Assign}}}

Now, for the induction step, we suppose the claim holds for sets of size
n
, and that
S=\{v_{1},\ldots,n_{n+1}\}
. Let
S^{\prime}=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\}
and note that:

x\leftarrow S\qquad=\qquad(x\leftarrow S^{\prime})\mathbin{\&}(x\coloneqq v_{n+1})

We can now complete the derivation as follows:

\frac{\frac{\;}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x\leftarrow S^{\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\textstyle\bignd_{i=1}^{n}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x=v_{n}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\quad\textsc{right={Induction Hypothesis}}\quad\frac{\;}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x\coloneqq v_{n+1}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x=v_{n+1}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\quad\textsc{Right=\hyperlink{rule:Assign}{{Assign}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~(x\leftarrow S^{\prime})\mathbin{\&}(x\coloneqq v_{n+1})~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}(\textstyle\bignd_{i=1}^{n}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x=v_{n}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil)\mathbin{\&}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x=v_{n+1}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\quad\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Nondet}{{Nondet}}}

∎

Appendix D
Analyzing Loops

D.1.
The Zero-One Law

Definition D.1 (Minimum Termination Probability).

Let the minimum termination probability of some set
S\in\mathcal{C}(X)
be defined as:

\mathsf{minterm}(S)\triangleq\inf_{\mu\in S}(1-\mu(\bot))

The following lemma (similar to Lemma 7.3.1 from
McIver and Morgan (2005)
) states that if some invariant holds for each iteration of a loop, then it will hold at the end of the program execution with probability at least
t
, where
t
is the minimum probability of termination for the loop.

Lemma D.2 (Invariant Reasoning).

Suppose that
\varphi\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
and
\psi\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lnot e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
and
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi\mathbin{\&}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
. Now take any
\mu\vDash\varphi
and let
t=\mathsf{minterm}(\left\llbracket\texttt{while}~e~\texttt{do}~C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}}))
be the minimum probability of termination. Then
\nu\vDash\psi\oplus_{t}\top
for any
\nu\in\left\llbracket\texttt{while}~e~\texttt{do}~C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}})
.

Proof.

Let:

F_{n}(S)\triangleq\Phi_{\langle C,e\rangle}^{\alpha}\left(\bot_{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}\right)^{\dagger}(S)

We begin by proving a more general claim: for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and any set
S\in\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)
such that
\nu\vDash\varphi
for all
\nu\in S
,
\xi\vDash\psi\oplus_{\mathsf{minterm}(F_{n}(S))}\top
for any
\xi\in F_{n}(S)
. The proof is by induction on
n
.

\triangleright

Base Case
. If
n=0
, then the claim holds trivially, since
F_{0}(S)=\mathord{\uparrow}\{\delta_{\bot}\}
and therefore
\mathsf{minterm}(F_{0}(S))=0
and so
\psi\oplus_{\mathsf{minterm}(F_{0}(S))}\top\Leftrightarrow\top
and anything satisfies
\top
.

\triangleright

Successor Case
. Suppose the claim holds for
n
. Now, we have:

\displaystyle F_{n+1}(S)

\displaystyle=\Phi_{\langle C,e\rangle}^{n+1}\left(\bot_{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}\right)^{\dagger}(S)

Note that since
\nu\vDash\varphi
for all
\nu\in S
, and therefore
\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(\sigma)=\mathsf{true}
for all
\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\nu)
, we can simplify as follows:

\displaystyle=\Phi_{\langle C,e\rangle}^{n}\left(\bot_{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}\right)^{\dagger}(\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(S))

By
Lemma

C.1
.

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{\nu\in\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(S)}\Phi_{\langle C,e\rangle}^{n}\left(\bot_{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}\right)^{\dagger}(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\nu\}})

Now, from
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi\mathbin{\&}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
, we know that for every
\nu\in\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}_{\bot}(S)
there is a probability
p
such that
\nu=p\cdot\nu_{1}+(1-p)\cdot\nu_{2}
and
\nu_{1}\vDash\varphi
and
\nu_{2}\vDash\psi
. Using
Lemma

C.4
, we get:

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{\nu_{1}\vDash\varphi,\nu_{2}\vDash\psi,p\mid p\cdot\nu_{1}+(1-p)\cdot\nu_{2}\in\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(S)}\Phi_{\langle C,e\rangle}^{n}\left(\bot_{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}\right)^{\dagger}_{\bot}(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\nu_{1}\}}\oplus_{p}\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\nu_{2}\}})

By
Lemma

C.2
.

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{\nu_{1}\vDash\varphi,\nu_{2}\vDash\psi,p\mid p\cdot\nu_{1}+(1-p)\cdot\nu_{2}\in\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(S)}\Phi_{\langle C,e\rangle}^{n}\left(\bot_{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}\right)^{\dagger}(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\nu_{1}\}})\oplus_{p}\Phi_{\langle C,e\rangle}^{n}\left(\bot_{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}\right)^{\dagger}_{\bot}(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\nu_{2}\}})

Since
\nu_{2}\vDash\psi
and
\psi\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lnot e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
, we know that applying
\Phi_{\langle C,e\rangle}^{n}\left(\bot_{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}\right)
to
\nu_{2}
will have no effect.

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{\nu_{1}\vDash\varphi,\nu_{2}\vDash\psi,p\mid p\cdot\nu_{1}+(1-p)\cdot\nu_{2}\in\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(S)}F_{n}(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\nu_{1}\}})\oplus_{p}\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\nu_{2}\}}

So, for any
\xi\in F_{n+1}
, we know that
\xi=p\cdot\xi^{\prime}+(1-p)\cdot\nu_{2}
such that
p\cdot\nu_{1}+(1-p)\cdot\nu_{2}\in\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(S)
and
\nu_{1}\vDash\varphi
and
\nu_{2}\vDash\psi
, and
\xi^{\prime}\in F_{n}(\nu_{1})
. By the induction hypothesis, we know that
\xi^{\prime}\vDash\psi\oplus_{\mathsf{minterm}(F_{n}(\{\nu_{1}\}))}\top
. Therefore,
\xi\vDash(\psi\oplus_{\mathsf{minterm}(F_{n}(\{\nu_{1}\}))}\top)\oplus_{p}\psi
and so
\xi\vDash\psi\oplus_{p\cdot\mathsf{minterm}(F_{n}(\{\nu_{1}\}))+(1-p)}\top
.

It remains only to show that:

\displaystyle\mathsf{minterm}(F_{n+1}(S))

\displaystyle=\inf_{\xi\in F_{n+1}(S)}(1-\xi(\bot))

\displaystyle=\inf_{\xi\in\bigcup_{\nu\in\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(S)}F_{n}(\{\nu_{1}\})\oplus_{p}\{\nu_{2}\}}(1-\xi(\bot))

\displaystyle=\inf_{\nu\in\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(S)}\inf_{\xi\in F_{\alpha}(\{\nu_{1}\})\oplus_{p}\{\nu_{2}\}}(1-\xi(\bot))

Since we know that
\bot\notin\mathsf{supp}(\nu_{2})

\displaystyle=\inf_{\nu\in\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(S)}\inf_{\xi\in F_{n}(\{\nu_{1}\})}(p\cdot(1-\xi(\bot))+1-p)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\nu\in\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(S)}p\cdot(\inf_{\xi\in F_{n}(\{\nu_{1}\})}(1-\xi(\bot)))+1-p

\displaystyle=\inf_{\nu\in\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(S)}p\cdot\mathsf{minterm}(F_{n}(\{\nu_{1}\}))+1-p

\displaystyle\leq p\cdot\mathsf{minterm}(F_{n}(\{\nu_{1}\}))+1-p

Now, since
\left\llbracket\texttt{while}~e~\texttt{do}~C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}})=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}F_{n}(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}})=\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}F_{n}(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}})
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathsf{minterm}(\left\llbracket\texttt{while}~e~\texttt{do}~C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}}))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{minterm}(\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}F_{n}(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}}))

\displaystyle=\inf_{\xi\in\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}F_{n}(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}})}(1-\xi(\bot))

Now since the
F_{n}(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}})
terms form a chain, the probability of
\bot
decreases monotonically as
n
increases:

\displaystyle=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\inf_{\xi\in F_{n}(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}})}(1-\xi(\bot))

\displaystyle=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathsf{minterm}(F_{n}(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}}))

Now, take any
\xi\in\left\llbracket\texttt{while}~e~\texttt{do}~C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}})
. We know that
\xi\in F_{n}(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}})
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and therefore
\xi\vDash\psi\oplus_{\mathsf{minterm}(F_{n}(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}}))}\top
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Since we also know that
\mathsf{minterm}(\left\llbracket\texttt{while}~e~\texttt{do}~C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}}))=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathsf{minterm}(F_{n}(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}}))
, we get that
\xi\vDash\psi\oplus_{\mathsf{minterm}(\left\llbracket\texttt{while}~e~\texttt{do}~C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}}))}\top
.

∎

Building on the previous lemma, we will show that in fact the probability of termination can only be zero or one. More precisely, if there is any positive probability of termination, then the program must almost surely terminate.

Lemma D.3 (Almost Sure Termination).

Suppose that
\varphi\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
and
\psi\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lnot e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
and
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi\mathbin{\&}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
and there exists some probability
p>0
such that
\mathsf{minterm}(\left\llbracket\texttt{while}~e~\texttt{do}~C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}}))\geq p
for any
\mu\vDash\varphi
.
Then, for any
\mu\vDash\varphi
,
\mathsf{minterm}(\left\llbracket\texttt{while}~e~\texttt{do}~C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}}))=1
.

Proof.

Given that
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi\mathbin{\&}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
, then after each execution of the loop there is some probability of ending up in a state satisfying
\varphi
, so that the loop will continue. Call each probability in this sequence
q_{\alpha}
, so the total probability of nontermination is:

\mathbb{P}[\text{nonterm}]\qquad=\qquad q_{1}\times q_{2}\times q_{3}\times\cdots\times q_{\alpha}\times q_{\alpha+1}\times\cdots

Each time the execution returns to a distribution
\mu\vDash\varphi
, we know that:

\mathsf{minterm}(\left\llbracket\texttt{while}~e~\texttt{do}~C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}}))\geq p

So, any tail of the product above must be at most
1-p
.

\mathbb{P}[\text{nonterm}]\qquad=\qquad q_{1}\times q_{2}\times q_{3}\times\cdots\times\underbrace{q_{\alpha}\times q_{\alpha+1}\times\cdots}_{\leq 1-p}

Let
\rho_{k}=\prod^{k}_{i=1}q_{i}
, the
{k}^{\text{th}}
partial product.
Since the
\rho_{k}
are monotonically decreasing and bounded below by
0
, we have that
\lim_{k\to\infty}\rho_{k}
exists, ensuring that the infinite product above exists.

Set
\rho=\lim_{k\to\infty}\rho_{k}=\mathbb{P}[\text{nonterm}]
.
We shall show that
\rho=0
, by contradiction.
Suppose that
\rho>0
.
Then, taking logarithms, we can convert the infinite product into a (converging) infinite series.
Setting
s_{k}=\log(\rho_{k})
and
s=\log(\rho)
, then we have

s_{k}=\log\left(\prod^{k}_{i=1}q_{i}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\log(q_{i})

Since
\log
is continuous,
\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}s_{k}=s
.
Similarly from the fact that the tail products of the
q_{i}
are always bounded by
1-p
, and the fact that
\log
is monotone, we have that for all
j
,

(8)

\sum^{\infty}_{i=j}\log(q_{i})\leq\log(1-p)

Let
\epsilon=|\log(1-p)|=-\log(1-p)

By convergence of
s_{k}
, there exists some
N
such that for all
m,n>N
,
|s_{m}-s_{n}|<\epsilon/2
.
Taking limits as
m\to\infty
, we have then that for
n>N
,
|s-s_{n}|\leq\epsilon/2<\epsilon
. Hence,

\displaystyle-\log(1-p)=\epsilon

\displaystyle>\left|s-s_{n}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\log(q_{i})-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log(q_{i})\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{i=n+1}^{\infty}\log(q_{i})\right|

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i=n+1}^{\infty}\log(q_{i})

Thus the above implies that
\log(1-p)<\sum_{i=n}^{\infty}\log(q_{i})
, contradicting
eq.

8
.
∎

Lemma D.4 (Zero One Law).

The following inference is valid:

\frac{\varphi\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\quad\psi\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lnot e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi\mathbin{\&}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{while}~e~\texttt{do}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lnot e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{p}\top{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\quad p>0}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{while}~e~\texttt{do}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{\hyperlink{rule:Zero-One}{\textsc{Zero-One}}}

Proof.

Suppose that
\mu\vDash\varphi
and take any
\nu\in\left\llbracket\texttt{while}~e~\texttt{do}~C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}})
. By
Lemma

D.2
, we know that
\nu\vDash\psi\oplus_{t}\top
where
t=\mathsf{minterm}(\left\llbracket\texttt{while}~e~\texttt{do}~C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu\}}))
.
Using
Lemma

D.3
and the premise that
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{while}~e~\texttt{do}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lnot e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{p}\top{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
, we know that
t=1
, therefore
\nu\vDash\psi
.
∎

D.2.
Variants and Ranking Functions

Theorem D.5 (The Bounded Integer Variant Rule).

Let
p>0
be some nonzero probability and
(\varphi_{n})_{n=0}^{N}
be a finite sequence of assertions such that
\varphi_{0}\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lnot e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
and
\varphi_{n}\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
for all
n\geq 1
.

\frac{\forall n\in[1,N].\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{n}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\textstyle(\bignd_{k=0}^{n-1}\varphi_{k})\oplus_{p}(\bignd_{k=0}^{N}\varphi_{k}){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\textstyle\bignd_{k=0}^{N}\varphi_{k}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{while}~e~\texttt{do}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{0}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{\textnormal{\hyperlink{rule:Bounded Variant}{{Bounded Variant}}}}

Proof.

This rule is a straightforward application of the
Zero-One
law.
Let
\varphi=\varphi_{1}\mathbin{\&}\cdots\mathbin{\&}\varphi_{N}
and
\psi=\varphi_{0}
, then it is relatively easy to see that
\varphi\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
and
\psi\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lnot e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
and that:

\textstyle(\bignd_{k=0}^{n-1}\varphi_{k})\oplus_{p}(\bignd_{k=0}^{N}\varphi_{k})\quad\Rightarrow\quad\textstyle(\bignd_{k=0}^{n-1}\varphi_{k})\mathbin{\&}(\bignd_{k=0}^{N}\varphi_{k})\quad\Rightarrow\quad\bignd_{k=0}^{N}\varphi_{k}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\varphi\mathbin{\&}\psi

So, we have:

\frac{\forall n\in[1,N].\quad\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{n}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\textstyle(\bignd_{k=0}^{n-1}\varphi_{k})\oplus_{p}(\bignd_{k=0}^{N}\varphi_{k}){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{n}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi\mathbin{\&}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\quad\textsc{Right=\hyperlink{rule:Consequence}{{Consequence}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi\mathbin{\&}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\quad\textsc{right={\hyperlink{rule:ND Split}{{ND Split}}~$\times~N$}}

So,
\varphi
and
\psi
are an invariant pair for
C
, giving us the first premise for the
Zero-One
law. Now, it just remains to show that there is a nonzero probability of termination. This is simple, since with probability
p
, the index of the variant must decrease by at least 1 on each iteration, so after
N
iterations the program must terminate with probability at least
p^{N}
. Since
p
is nonzero and
N
is finite, then
p^{N}
is also finite, so we have.

\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{while}~e~\texttt{do}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lnot e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{p^{N}}\top{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}

∎

We now show how to derive the bounded integer rule of
McIver and Morgan (2005, Lemma 7.5.1)
and
Kaminski (2019, Theorem 6.7)
.

Lemma D.6.

Let
P
be some basic assertion (the invariant),
e_{\mathsf{rank}}
be an integer-valued ranking expression, and
p>0
be some nonzero probability. Also, suppose that
\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\ell\leq e_{\mathsf{rank}}\leq h\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
. Then, the following inference rule is derivable:

\frac{\forall z\in[\ell,h].\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land e\land e_{\mathsf{rank}}=z\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\land e_{\mathsf{rank}}<z\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{p}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{while}~e~\texttt{do}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\land\lnot e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{\textnormal{\hyperlink{rule:Bounded Rank}{{Bounded Rank}}}}

Proof.

Let
N=h-\ell+1
, and let
(\varphi_{n})_{n=0}^{N}
be defined as follows:

\varphi_{n}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land\lnot e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil&\text{if}~n=0\\
\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land e\land e_{\mathsf{rank}}=\ell+n-1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil&\text{if}~n\in[1,N]\end{array}\right.

First, we argue that
\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\Rightarrow\varphi_{0}\mathbin{\&}\cdots\mathbin{\&}\varphi_{N}
. Suppose that
\mu\vDash\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
, so
\sigma\in\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis
for each
\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)
. It must be that
\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(\sigma)
is either
\mathsf{true}
or
\mathsf{false}
. If it is
\mathsf{true}
, then we also know that
\ell\leq\left\llbracket e_{\mathsf{rank}}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\leq h
since
\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\ell\leq e_{\mathsf{rank}}\leq h\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
, and therefore
\eta(\sigma)\vDash\varphi_{n}=\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land e\land e_{\mathsf{rank}}=\ell+n-1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
where
n=\left\llbracket e_{\mathsf{rank}}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)-\ell+1
. Now suppose that
\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(\sigma)=\mathsf{false}
, then clearly
\eta(\sigma)\vDash\varphi_{0}=\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land\lnot e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
. Since this is true for all
\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)
, we have:

\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\quad\Rightarrow\quad\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\land\lnot e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\mathbin{\&}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\land e\land e_{\mathsf{rank}}=\ell\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\mathbin{\&}\cdots\mathbin{\&}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\land e\land e_{\mathsf{rank}}=h\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\quad=\quad\varphi_{0}\mathbin{\&}\cdots\mathbin{\&}\varphi_{N}

Next, we show that
\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land e_{\mathsf{rank}}<\ell+n-1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\Rightarrow\varphi_{0}\mathbin{\&}\cdots\mathbin{\&}\varphi_{n-1}
. Suppose that
\mu\vDash\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land e_{\mathsf{rank}}<\ell+n-1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
, meaning that
\sigma\in\llparenthesis P\land e_{\mathsf{rank}}<\ell+n-1\rrparenthesis
for each
\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)
. If
\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(\sigma)=\mathsf{true}
, then we know that
\ell\leq\left\llbracket e_{\mathsf{rank}}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\leq h
since
\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\ell\leq e_{\mathsf{rank}}\leq h\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
. We also know that
\left\llbracket e_{\mathsf{rank}}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)<\ell+n-1
, so there must be some
m<n
such that
\left\llbracket e_{\mathsf{rank}}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)=\ell+m-1
, so
\eta(\sigma)\vDash\varphi_{m}
. If instead
\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(\sigma)=\mathsf{false}
, then
\eta(\sigma)\vDash\varphi_{0}
. Therefore,
\mu\vDash\varphi_{0}\mathbin{\&}\cdots\mathbin{\&}\varphi_{n-1}
.

We now complete the derivation as follows:

\frac{\forall n\in[1,N].\quad\frac{\frac{\forall z\in[\ell,h].\hskip 7.3612pt{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land e\land e_{\mathsf{rank}}=z\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\land e_{\mathsf{rank}}<z\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{p}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land e\land e_{\mathsf{rank}}=\ell+n-1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\land e_{\mathsf{rank}}<\ell+n-1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{p}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land e\land e_{\mathsf{rank}}=\ell+n-1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\textstyle(\bignd_{k=0}^{n-1}\varphi_{k})\oplus_{p}(\bignd_{k=0}^{N}\varphi_{k}){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\quad\textsc{Right=\hyperlink{rule:Consequence}{{Consequence}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{while}~e~\texttt{do}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\land\lnot e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\quad\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Bounded Variant}{{Bounded Variant}}}

∎

D.3.
The Progressing Rank Rule

In this section, we present our propositional variant of a more complex and powerful almost sure termination rule that was originally developed by
McIver
et al. (2018)
and later refined by
Kaminski (2019, §6.2.3)
. Similar to the
Bounded Rank
rule, the new rule relies on an invariant
P
and a ranking function
e_{\mathsf{rank}}
. However, this time
e_{\mathsf{rank}}
is unbounded and is nonnegative-rational-valued rather than integer-valued. In addition, two more antitone functions are needed as well:

\displaystyle\left\llbracket e_{\mathsf{rank}}\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}

(Ranking Function)

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle\colon\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}\to(0,1]

(Probability)

\displaystyle d

\displaystyle\colon\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}

(Decrease)

Antitonicity means that
p(r)\geq p(s)
if
r\leq s
, and the same for
d
. Additionally, the loop guard
e
must be false iff
e_{\mathsf{rank}}=0
.

The intuition behind
p
and
d
is that as the rank gets closer to
0
(implying termination), the probability of decreasing the rank and the magnitude of such a decrease both increase. The premise of the rule is that if the rank is initially
k
, then after one iteration of the loop, rank must decrease by at lease
d(k)
with probability at least
p(k)
. In addition, the expected rank must decrease monotonically as well. The rule is shown below:

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land e\land e_{\mathsf{rank}}=k\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\land e_{\mathsf{rank}}\leq k-d(k)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{p(k)}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\land e_{\mathsf{rank}}\leq k+{\textstyle\frac{p(k)}{1-p(k)}}d(k)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{while}~e~\texttt{do}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\land\lnot e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{\hyperlink{rule:Progressing Rank}{\textsc{Progressing Rank}}}

Note that the expected decrease in rank is guaranteed by the fact that there are two probabilistic outcomes, and in the weighted average we have
e_{\mathsf{rank}}\leq k
(more on that soon, when we discuss soundness).

The weakest pre-expectation version of this rule presented in
Kaminski (2019, §6.2.3)
has 3 different premises, which involve different concepts such as
sub-invariants
and
super-invariants
, as well as both the
demonic
and
angelic

\mathsf{wp}
calculi. We contend that the Outcome Logic style version presented here is simpler because all three of those premises have been coalesced, using just a single concept:
demonic outcome triples
.

Soundness of the Rule

We first establish that
\varphi\triangleq\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
and
\psi\triangleq\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land\lnot e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
is an invariant pair for the program. That is, we must prove that
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\land e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\mathbin{\&}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\land\lnot e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
is a valid triple.

Suppose that
\mu\vDash\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
. Then we can partition
\mu
into a countable number of components based on the value of
e_{\mathsf{rank}}
. That is, there must be some distribution
\xi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0})
and distributions
(\mu_{k})_{k\in\mathsf{supp}(\xi)}
such that
\mu={\sum_{k\in\mathsf{supp}(\xi)}}\xi(k)\cdot\mu_{k}
and
\left\llbracket e_{\mathsf{rank}}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)=k
for each
k\in\mathsf{supp}(\xi)
and
\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu_{k})
. Clearly, we have
\mu_{k}\vDash\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land e\land e_{\mathsf{rank}}=k\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
for each
k\in\mathsf{supp}(\xi)
, so by the premise of
Progressing Rank
, we know that:

\displaystyle\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\mathord{\uparrow}{\{\mu_{k}\}})\vDash

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land e_{\mathsf{rank}}\leq k-d(k)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{p(k)}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\land e_{\mathsf{rank}}\leq k+{\textstyle\frac{p(k)}{1-p(k)}}d(k)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\;\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\;\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\mathbin{\&}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\land\lnot e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil

Therefore, by
Lemma

C.5
,
\mu\vDash\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\mathbin{\&}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\land\lnot e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
and by
Lemma

D.2
,
\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land\lnot e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
applies to all the terminating outcomes. All that remains is to show that the program almost surely terminates. For this, we will appeal to
Kaminski (2019, Theorem 6.8)
, which relies on the weakest pre-expectation
\mathsf{wp}\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket(e)
as defined in
Kaminski (2019, §4.1)
and is equal to the minimum expected value of the expression
e
after running the program
C
:

(9)

\mathsf{wp}\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket(e)(\sigma)=\inf_{\mu\in\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket(\sigma)}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[e]\qquad\text{where}\qquad\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[e]\triangleq\sum_{\tau\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(\tau)\cdot\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(\tau)

In addition, let the Iverson brackets
[e]
evaluates to 0 if
e
is false and 1 if
e
is true. To invoke Theorem 6.8 of
Kaminski (2019)
, we must show that the four following premises hold:

a.

P
is a
wp
-subinvariant
(
Kaminski, 2019
, Definiton 5.1b)
of
\texttt{while}~e~\texttt{do}~C
:

[P]\leq[\lnot e]\cdot[P]+[e]\cdot\mathsf{wp}\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket([P])

If
e
is false, then this reduces to
[P]\leq[P]
, which is trivially true. If not, it reduces to
[P]\leq\mathsf{wp}\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket([P])
, which is implied by the fact the
P
is an invariant of the loop, as we just showed.

b.

e_{\mathsf{rank}}=0
implies termination. This was an assumption.

c.

e_{\mathsf{rank}}
is an
awp
-superinvariant
(
Kaminski, 2019
, Definiton 5.1c)
of
\texttt{while}~e~\texttt{do}~C
:

e_{\mathsf{rank}}\geq[\lnot e]\cdot e_{\mathsf{rank}}+[e]\cdot\mathsf{awp}\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket(e_{\mathsf{rank}})

If
e
is false, then this reduces to
e_{\mathsf{rank}}\geq e_{\mathsf{rank}}
, which is trivially true. If not, then it reduces to
e_{\mathsf{rank}}\geq\mathsf{awp}\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket(e_{\mathsf{rank}})
. Here
\mathsf{awp}
is the
angelic
weakest pre, which maximizes expected values instead of minimizing them. Formally, the definition is the same as (
9
), but with the
\inf
replaced by a
\sup
. It is used since we are now bounding the value from above. This condition simply states that the expected value of
e_{\mathsf{rank}}
must not increase after executing the loop body.

To show that this is true, take any state
\sigma\in\llparenthesis P\land e\rrparenthesis
and let
k=\left\llbracket e_{\mathsf{rank}}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\sigma)
3
3

3

Note that condition
c
does not require
P
to hold, however we can modify
e_{\mathsf{rank}}
such that it is equal to the constant 0 whenever
P
is false without affecting the truth of any of our other claims.

.
We know from the premise of
Progressing Rank
that
\mu\vDash\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land e_{\mathsf{rank}}\leq k-d(k)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{p(k)}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\land e_{\mathsf{rank}}\leq k+{\textstyle\frac{p(k)}{1-p(k)}}d(k)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
for any
\mu\in\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket(\sigma)
. That means that there are
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
such that
\mu=p(k)\cdot\mu_{1}+(1-p(k))\cdot\mu_{2}
and:

\mathsf{supp}(\mu_{1})\subseteq\llparenthesis P\land e_{\mathsf{rank}}\leq k-d(k)\rrparenthesis\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{supp}(\mu_{2})\subseteq\llparenthesis P\land e_{\mathsf{rank}}\leq k+{\textstyle\frac{p(k)}{1-p(k)}}d(k)\rrparenthesis

From this, we can determine the expected value of
e_{\mathsf{rank}}
according to
\mu
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[e_{\mathsf{rank}}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(\tau)\cdot\left\llbracket e_{\mathsf{rank}}\right\rrbracket(\tau)

\displaystyle=p(k)\cdot\sum_{\tau\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu_{1})}\mu_{1}(\tau)\cdot\left\llbracket e_{\mathsf{rank}}\right\rrbracket(\tau)+(1-p(k))\cdot\sum_{\tau\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu_{2})}\mu_{2}(\tau)\cdot\left\llbracket e_{\mathsf{rank}}\right\rrbracket(\tau)

\displaystyle\leq p(k)\cdot\sum_{\tau\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu_{1})}\mu_{1}(\tau)\cdot(k-d(k))+(1-p(k))\cdot\sum_{\tau\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu_{2})}\mu_{2}(\tau)\cdot\left(k+{\frac{p(k)}{1-p(k)}}\cdot d(k)\right)

Since
|\mu_{1}|=|\mu_{2}|=1
:

\displaystyle=p(k)\cdot(k-d(k))+(1-p(k))\cdot\left(k+{\frac{p(k)}{1-p(k)}}\cdot d(k)\right)

\displaystyle=(p(k)+1-p(k))\cdot k-p(k)\cdot d(k)+\cancel{(1-p(k))}\cdot\frac{p(k)}{\cancel{1-p(k)}}\cdot d(k)

\displaystyle=k

So, we have shown that
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[e_{\mathsf{rank}}]\leq k
for all
\mu\in\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket(\sigma)
, therefore the premise that the expected value must always decrease holds.

d.

e_{\mathsf{rank}}
satisfies a progress condition; for any
k\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
:

p(k)\cdot[e]\cdot[P]\cdot[e_{\mathsf{rank}}=k]\leq\mathsf{wp}\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket([e_{\mathsf{rank}}\leq k-d(k)])

This condition simply states that if
\sigma\in\llparenthesis P\land e\land e_{\mathsf{rank}}=k\rrparenthesis
, then
e_{\mathsf{rank}}\leq k-d(k)
holds after running
C
with probability at least
p(k)
, which is implied directly by the premise of
Progressing Rank
.

Demonically Fair Random Walk

We can prove almost sure termination of a more sophisticated random walk using the
Progressing Rank
rule. This example is adapted from
McIver
et al. (2018, §5.2)
and
Kaminski (2019, §6.2.4.1)
.
The program below represents a random walk in which the agent steps towards the origin with probability
\frac{1}{2}
, otherwise the adversary can choose whether or not to step away from the origin.

\begin{array}[]{l}\texttt{while}~x>0~\texttt{do}\\
\quad x\coloneqq x-1\oplus_{\frac{1}{2}}(x\coloneqq x+1\mathbin{\&}\texttt{skip})\end{array}

Now, we can instantiate the
Progressing Rank
rule by letting:

P\triangleq x\geq 0\qquad\qquad e_{\mathsf{rank}}\triangleq x\qquad\qquad p(k)\triangleq\frac{1}{2}\qquad\qquad d(k)\triangleq 1

In addition, we will presume that
x
is always integer-valued.
We now prove the premise of
Progressing Rank
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x>0\land x=k\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\left(\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x>0\land x=k\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;x\coloneqq x-1\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x\geq 0\land x=k-1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\end{array}\right)\oplus_{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x>0\land x=k\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;x\coloneqq x+1\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x\geq 0\land x=k+1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\end{array}\mathbin{\&}\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x>0\land x=k\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;\texttt{skip}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x\geq 0\land x=k\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\end{array}\right)\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x\geq 0\land x=k-1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{2}}(\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x\geq 0\land x=k+1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\mathbin{\&}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x\geq 0\land x=k\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x\geq 0\land x\leq k-1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{2}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x\geq 0\land x\leq k+1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\end{array}

The consequence in the last step is justified since we can weaken the second nondeterministic possibility to be
x\leq k+1
and then use idempotence of
\mathbin{\&}
to collapse the two possibilities.
After applying
Progressing Rank
, we get the following triple:

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x\geq 0\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{while}~x>0~\texttt{do}~x\coloneqq x-1\oplus_{\frac{1}{2}}(x\coloneqq x+1\mathbin{\&}\texttt{skip})~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x=0\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}

Appendix E
Probabilistic SAT Solving by Partial Rejection Sampling

In this section, we provide the technical details that were omitted from the main text in
Section

6.3
.

E.1.
Semantics of Lists

Before showing formal derivations for the subroutines, we discuss the semantics of lists used in the case study. A list is a finite sequence of values
\langle v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\rangle
. In order to manipulate lists in programs, we add the following expression syntax:

e\Coloneqq\cdots\mid\langle e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}\rangle\mid e_{1}[e_{2}]\mid e_{1}[e_{2}\mapsto e_{3}]

The three new additions are list literals
\langle e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}\rangle
, list accesses
e_{1}[e_{2}]
(where
e_{1}
is a list and
e_{2}
is the index of the element being lookup up), and list updates
e_{1}[e_{2}\mapsto e_{3}]
, which create a new list from
e_{1}
with index
e_{2}
now having value
e_{3}
.
The semantics of these expressions is below.

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\langle e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}\rangle\right\rrbracket(\sigma)

\displaystyle\triangleq\langle\left\llbracket e_{1}\right\rrbracket(\sigma),\ldots,\left\llbracket e_{n}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\rangle

\displaystyle\left\llbracket e_{1}[e_{2}]\right\rrbracket(\sigma)

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}v_{k}&\text{if}~\left\llbracket e_{1}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)=\langle v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\rangle~\text{and}~\left\llbracket e_{2}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)=k~\text{and}~1\leq k\leq n\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\left\llbracket e_{1}[e_{2}\mapsto e_{3}]\right\rrbracket(\sigma)

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\langle v_{1},\ldots,v_{\max(k,n)}\rangle&\text{if}~\left\llbracket e_{1}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)=\langle v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\rangle\\
&\;\;\text{where}\left\llbracket e_{2}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)=k~\text{and}~v_{k}=\left\llbracket e_{3}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\\
&\;\;\text{and}~v_{i}=0~\text{for}~n<i<k\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

Note that list operations never fail. Looking up an invalid index simply evaluates to 0. Additionally, if an update is out of bounds, then the intermediary values get filled in with zeros.
In addition, we provide some syntactic sugar in order to update lists in the style of arrays.

\begin{array}[]{lcll}x[&e_{1}&]\coloneqq e_{2}&\qquad\triangleq\qquad x\coloneqq x[e_{1}\mapsto e_{2}]\\
x[&e&]\coloneqq\mathsf{flip}(p)&\qquad\triangleq\qquad(x[e]\coloneqq\mathsf{true})\oplus_{p}(x[e]\coloneqq\mathsf{false})\end{array}

Derivation

. Provided that the formula is not yet satisfied,
