{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1\leq s\leq M\land\texttt{EvalClause}(s)=\mathsf{false}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\end{array}

Figure 9
.
Derivation of the
SelectClause
subroutine.

Termination of the loop is deterministic, but we can still analyze it using the
Bounded Rank
rule. We use the ranking function
e_{\mathsf{rank}}\triangleq M-i
so that as the iteration counter
i
goes from 1 to
M
, the rank decreases towards 0. In addition, this decrease certainly occurs each iteration, so we let
p\triangleq 1
. The invariant is defined below:

P\triangleq\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\texttt{Eval}=\mathsf{false}\land 1\leq i\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\land\bignd\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}s=-1\land\textstyle\bigwedge_{k=0}^{i-1}\texttt{EvalClause}(k)=\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\\
\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}1\leq s\leq M\land\texttt{EvalClause}(s)=\mathsf{false}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\end{array}\right.

The invariant has three components. First, it asserts that
\texttt{Eval}=\mathsf{false}
, meaning that the formula is not satisfied, which will guarantee that the subroutine can find an unsatisfied clause. Next,
1\leq i
, meaning that
i
is either in bounds. Finally, either
s=-1
and all the clauses up to
i
are satisfied, or
1\leq s\leq M
and clause
s
is not satisfied. Here, the
\mathbin{\&}
plays a similar role to a disjunction;
\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{1}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\mathbin{\&}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}Q_{2}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
says that each element in the support of the underlying distribution satisfies either
Q_{1}
or
Q_{2}
. Clearly
\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land i\leq M\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
implies that the rank is bounded between
0
and
M-1
:

\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land i\leq M\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\quad\Rightarrow\quad\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}1\leq i\leq M\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\quad\Rightarrow\quad\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}0\leq M-i\leq M-1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil

We now describe the derivation, shown in
Figure

9
. Upon entering the loop, we presume that
e_{\mathsf{rank}}=k
for some
1\leq k\leq M-1
, which also means that
i=M-k
. We can then split each of the two
\mathbin{\&}
-clauses into two parts depending on whether
\texttt{EvalClause}(i)
is true or false, since for any Boolean-valued expression
e
:

\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{1}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\mathbin{\&}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}Q_{2}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad(\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}Q_{1}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\mathbin{\&}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}Q_{2}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil)\land\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\quad\mathbin{\&}\quad(\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}Q_{1}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\mathbin{\&}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}Q_{2}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil)\land\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lnot e\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil

We then use
Lemma

C.9
to analyze the outer if statement. In the true branch, we again use
Lemma

C.9
to split into the outcomes where
s=-1
and
1\leq s\leq M
. If
s=-1
, then we assign
s\coloneqq i
, which establishes the second case of the invariant. If
s\neq-1
, then we nondeterministically choose to do either nothing or assign
s\coloneqq i
, both of which establish the second case of the invariant.

Nothing happens in the false branch of the outer if statement, although we do know in this case that
\texttt{EvalClause}(i)=\mathsf{true}
, so we can increment the upper limit of the conjunction in the first case of the invariant. Joining the two branches and incrementing
i
reestablishes the invariant and also decreases the rank.

Exiting the loop, we have
P\land i>M
, which makes the first case of the invariant impossible, since it would say that all the clauses are satisfied, despite the fact that
\texttt{Eval}=\mathsf{false}
. So, we know the second case of the invariant must hold for the entire support.

E.3.
SampleClause
Derivation

In this section, we give the derivation for the
SampleClause
subroutine, which decreases the Hamming distance between the current solution
\mathsf{x}
and the sample solution
\mathsf{x}^{\ast}
with probability at least
1/8
. The derivation is shown in
Figure

10
.

\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0\leq i<M\land\mathsf{dist}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{x}^{\ast})=k\land\texttt{EvalClause}(i)=\mathsf{false}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sum_{j\in J}\big[\mathsf{x}[j]\neq\mathsf{x}^{\ast}[j]\big]<k\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;\mathsf{x}[\mathsf{cv}[i][0]]\coloneq\mathsf{flip}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\right)\fatsemi\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sum_{j\in J}\big[\mathsf{x}[j]\neq\mathsf{x}^{\ast}[j]\big]<k\land\mathsf{x}[c_{0}]=\mathsf{x}^{\ast}[c_{0}]\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{2}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;\mathsf{x}[\mathsf{cv}[i][1]]\coloneq\mathsf{flip}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\right)\fatsemi\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sum_{j\in J}\big[\mathsf{x}[j]\neq\mathsf{x}^{\ast}[j]\big]<k\land\mathsf{x}[c_{0}]=\mathsf{x}^{\ast}[c_{0}]\land\mathsf{x}[c_{1}]=\mathsf{x}^{\ast}[c_{1}]\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{4}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;\mathsf{x}[\mathsf{cv}[i][2]]\coloneq\mathsf{flip}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\right)\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sum_{j\in J}\big[\mathsf{x}[j]\neq\mathsf{x}^{\ast}[j]\big]<k\land\mathsf{x}[c_{0}]=\mathsf{x}^{\ast}[c_{0}]\land\mathsf{x}[c_{1}]=\mathsf{x}^{\ast}[c_{1}]\land\mathsf{x}[c_{2}]=\mathsf{x}^{\ast}[c_{2}]\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{8}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{dist}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{x}^{\ast})<k\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{8}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\end{array}

Figure 10
.
Derivation for the
SampleClause
subroutine.

At the beginning, we presume that
1\leq i\leq M
,
\mathsf{dist}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{x}^{\ast})=k
, and
\texttt{EvalClause}(s)=\mathsf{false}
. Unfolding the definition of Hamming distance, we get
\sum_{j=1}^{N}\big[\mathsf{x}[j]\neq\mathsf{x}^{\ast}[j]\big]=k
.
Now let
c_{j}\triangleq\mathsf{cv}[s][j]
be the index of the
{j}^{\text{th}}
variable in the
{s}^{\text{th}}
clause and let
J\triangleq\{j\mid 1\leq j\leq N,j\notin\{c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}\}\}
be the set of indices of variables that do not appear in the
{s}^{\text{th}}
clause. Since
\texttt{EvalClause}(s)=\mathsf{false}
, it must be the case that
\mathsf{x}[c_{j}]\neq\mathsf{x}^{\ast}[c_{j}]
for some
j\in\{1,2,3\}
, therefore summing over
J
instead of over all the indices must yield a value strictly less than
k
.

Each coin flip gives us
\mathsf{x}[c_{j}]=\mathsf{x}^{\ast}[c_{j}]
with probability
\frac{1}{2}
, so all three are equal with probability
\frac{1}{8}
. A sketch of the details of those derivations is given below, where
Q
is the precondition of the command from
Figure

10
.

\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{\;}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q\land\mathsf{x}^{\ast}[e]=\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\mathsf{x}\coloneqq\mathsf{x}[e\mapsto\mathsf{true}]~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}Q\land\mathsf{x}[e]=\mathsf{x}^{\ast}[e]\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 7.3612pt\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Assign}{{Assign}}}\hskip 7.3612pt\vdots}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q\land\mathsf{x}^{\ast}[e]=\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\mathsf{x}[e]\coloneqq\mathsf{flip}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\right)~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}Q\land\mathsf{x}[e]=\mathsf{x}^{\ast}[e]\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{2}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Prob}{{Prob}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\vdots}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q\land\mathsf{x}^{\ast}[e]=\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\mathbin{\&}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}Q\land\mathsf{x}^{\ast}[e]=\mathsf{false}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\mathsf{x}[e]\coloneqq\mathsf{flip}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\right)~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}Q\land\mathsf{x}[e]=\mathsf{x}^{\ast}[e]\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{2}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\quad\textsc{Right=\hyperlink{rule:ND Split}{{ND Split}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\mathsf{x}[e]\coloneqq\mathsf{flip}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\right)~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}Q\land\mathsf{x}[e]=\mathsf{x}^{\ast}[e]\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{2}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\quad\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Consequence}{{Consequence}}}

The first step is to use the rule of
Consequence
to split into the two cases where
\mathsf{x}^{\ast}[e]
is either true or false—both lead to the same postcondition, which is collapsed using idempotence of
\mathbin{\&}
. Next, we use
ND Split
to analyze the program for each value of
\mathsf{x}^{\ast}[e]
. Since
\mathsf{x}[e]\coloneqq\mathsf{flip}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)
is syntactic sugar for a probabilistic choice between two assignments, the derivation is completed with
Prob
and
Assign
.

Note that the same variable could appear multiple times in the clause (
c_{j}=c_{\ell}=n
where
j\neq\ell
), but this does not affect our argument, since we only consider what happens when the coin flip matches
\mathsf{x}^{\ast}
. It does not matter if the first sample gave us
\mathsf{x}[n]\neq\mathsf{x}^{\ast}[n]
and then the second one gives us
\mathsf{x}[n]=\mathsf{x}^{\ast}[n]
, since all we said in that case is
\mathsf{true}
, which vacuously holds even if the variable is later resampled. As such,
\frac{1}{8}
is only a
lower bound
on reducing the Hamming distance, but it is a tight enough bound to prove almost sure termination.

At the end of the routine, on the left side of the
\oplus_{\frac{1}{8}}
, we have
\mathsf{x}[c_{j}]=\mathsf{x}^{\ast}[c_{j}]
for all
j\in\{1,2,3\}
, so expanding the bounds of the sum to be over all indices instead of over
J
does not affect its value, since we are just adding zeros. We therefore get that
\mathsf{dist}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{x}^{\ast})<k
with probability at least
\frac{1}{8}
.
